6 Distributed Training

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Fleet stack focus for this chapter.

Why can adding accelerators make training slower?

Splitting a training job across machines creates more compute capacity, but it also requires those machines to exchange state and agree on updates. Each accelerator may finish its local work sooner while spending more of the step waiting for other workers. Once communication and waiting consume the time saved by parallel execution, adding accelerators raises the bill without shortening the run. Single-node optimization remains necessary, yet faster local arithmetic cannot remove a dependency on data that another machine has not delivered. Memory pressure complicates the decision, because a model may require distribution simply to fit, even when the resulting exchanges are expensive. Dividing its state across more devices can relieve that limit while exposing another in network bandwidth or scheduling. Conversely, replicating a model can process more examples at once without reducing the memory needed for each copy. Neither improvement alone establishes that training will finish sooner. Changing how examples are grouped or when updates become available can change the work required to reach the target quality. Engineers must therefore compare complete training configurations, accounting for memory, communication, update behavior, and elapsed time together. The practical question is where additional hardware buys useful progress and where it merely creates more coordination. That judgment determines which models and experiments fit the available schedule and budget. In C³ terms, each partitioning strategy trades parallelized compute against network-bound communication and coordination across workers.

Learning Objectives
  • Apply the Fleet Law to diagnose compute-, memory-, communication-, or coordination-bound distributed training regimes
  • Calculate scaling efficiency, estimate useful batch size, and budget synchronization overhead for data-parallel training
  • Design memory-sharding plans that trade accelerator capacity for additional communication
  • Map tensor, pipeline, sequence, expert, and data parallelism onto hardware bandwidth tiers and model structure
  • Analyze microbatch pipeline schedules for bubble overhead, activation residency, and synchronous update ordering
  • Evaluate synchronization and low-precision policies under staleness, straggler, and numerical-stability constraints
  • Synthesize hybrid parallelism configurations for frontier, recommendation, and alignment training constraints
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

6.1 Why Distribution Is Necessary

A 175B-parameter reference model cannot load onto a single accelerator, because its FP16 weights alone exceed the memory of the device that must hold them (Data Storage). The empirical scaling law (principle 9) keeps pushing frontier models further past what one machine can store or finish in calendar time. The accelerator hierarchy, network fabric, and storage pipeline form the physical foundation of the fleet, and Collective Communication priced the collective operations that move state across it. The remaining challenge is deciding how to partition the training job: which intermediate results and gradients must cross that fabric, and when workers must synchronize, while preserving the optimization process a single device would have followed.

↩︎ The power-law relationship between quality and compute, data, and parameters, and the compute-optimal allocation it implies, are established in AI Scaling Laws.

In the fleet stack (The Fleet Stack), distributed training occupies the Distributed Execution row of the Distribution band, the logic that partitions the mathematical workload across the physical fleet. Data, tensor, pipeline, and hybrid parallelism create different traffic patterns for the interconnect to carry, and selecting among them requires evaluating the reference workload against finite high-bandwidth memory (HBM) capacity, compute throughput, and input bandwidth.

Three distinct limits push a training workload past a single accelerator. Memory exhaustion hits first when model parameters, optimizer states, and activation storage exceed single-device capacity. For full mixed-precision training with Adaptive Moment Estimation (Adam), the convention of 16 bytes per parameter can exceed an 80 GB accelerator at roughly 5 billion parameters before activations. Larger 10–20B models therefore need sharding, offload, or another memory-saving approach (Rajbhandari et al. 2020). The failed allocation establishes a capacity problem without by itself choosing the remedy.

↪ Accelerator HBM capacity, the ceiling these per-parameter budgets are measured against, is developed in Accelerator Memory Hierarchy.

Even when a model fits in memory, unacceptable training duration can still force distribution because a single device may require weeks or months to converge, turning wall-clock time itself into a systems constraint. GPT-3’s 175B-parameter training run used a cluster of V100 GPUs (Brown et al. 2020), illustrating why the calendar, alongside HBM capacity, drives scale-out.

Input demand at dataset scale adds a third pressure: dataset bytes govern storage capacity, whereas the training schedule governs the required delivery rate. As The Data Pipeline Equation showed, either can motivate distributed data services, although a large dataset alone does not require distributing model computation. Together, capacity, calendar, and input delivery define the constraints a training configuration must satisfy.

The first signal, memory exhaustion, has a long history, traced in figure 1. Over the plotted span, single-accelerator memory grew about 15× from the K80 to the B200, while the memory needed to train a state-of-the-art model grew by roughly 4 orders of magnitude. Among the plotted models, the curves cross in 2020 at GPT-3, whose training state exceeded the largest accelerator then available by about 35×. GPT-3 marks where the gap passed an order of magnitude, and every later plotted frontier model also sits far above the capacity curve. For those models, spreading state beyond one device’s memory is a precondition for training at all rather than a way to train faster.

Whichever signal motivates distribution, the system must state which optimization update it implements. The synchronous baseline is developed first. In it, the 1,024 GPUs of the volume’s Archetype A (GPT-4/Llama-3) reference cluster combine their contributions before advancing to the next parameter version. The running instance in this chapter is a GPT-3-class 175B-parameter dense transformer. Matching the global batch, loss normalization, and optimizer state preserves the corresponding single-device update mathematically. Later synchronization models deliberately relax that update ordering and must be evaluated under their own convergence assumptions.

↩︎ The three systems archetypes and their binding constraints are established in Three systems archetypes.

Definition 6.1: Distributed training

Distributed training partitions the optimization loop across multiple compute nodes, distributing data or model computation and coordinating contributions according to a specified update protocol.

  1. Significance: Partitioning training state across devices can meet a memory budget that exceeds one accelerator. GPT-3 (175B parameters) requires approximately 350 GB in BF16 (FP16 also stores 2 bytes per value, so the chapter’s byte counts hold for either format), more than 4× the 80 GB capacity of a single H100. If weights remain entirely in HBM, their aggregate capacity lower bound is 5 H100s. Adding BF16 gradients plus FP32 Adam master weights and moments gives about 2.8 TB, or at least 35 H100s before activations. These lower bounds do not establish a runnable placement; offload changes the capacity calculation, and the time budget can require more accelerators than memory alone.
  2. Distinction: CPU threads can share a coherent address space, while accelerators generally hold separate training-state allocations even within one node. Distributed training extends coordination across those devices and node boundaries through collectives or point-to-point transfers governed by the chosen update protocol.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that distributed training scales linearly with node count. In practice, communication overhead grows with cluster size and the serial fraction of each step (Amdahl’s Law): a fixed serial fraction of the single-device baseline bounds strong-scaling speedup.

Distribution expands three costs that determine whether a partition is viable. Communication overhead includes gradient aggregation through AllReduce, which returns the same reduced tensor to each participant, or rank. For \(\Parameters\) parameters across \(\DeviceCount\) devices, a ring AllReduce, in which each worker passes gradient chunks only to its neighbor in a logical ring, sends approximately \(2(\DeviceCount-1)/\DeviceCount\times \GradientPayload\) bytes per worker per step. Here the gradient payload \(\GradientPayload\) is the \(\Parameters\) gradient values times their storage width. That payload is not total fleet traffic.

↩︎ The ring schedule and the derivation of its \(2(\DeviceCount-1)/\DeviceCount\) volume are established in Collective Communication.

Fault tolerance becomes more demanding as the number of failure sources grows. Under independent constant-rate failures, 1,024 GPUs that each have a mean time to failure (MTTF) of 50,000 hours produce a GPU failure about every 49 hours, the cluster’s mean time between failures (MTBF). Whether each event interrupts training depends on recovery and redundancy; The MTBF cascade develops the assumptions.

Algorithmic stability adds a separate constraint when the partition changes global batch size or update ordering. Learning-rate scaling and warmup are candidate responses to large-batch behavior (Goyal et al. 2017), not requirements unique to multiple machines. A viable design must satisfy all three budgets.

Communication and coordination costs enter every step through the Fleet Law (principle 10), which decomposes the per-step time:

\[ \DistributedStepTime(\DeviceCount) = \frac{\IronLawComputeTime}{\DeviceCount} + \CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount) + \SynchronizationTime(\DeviceCount) - \OverlappedTime \]

↩︎ The Fleet Law and its compute, communication, and coordination terms are established in The Fleet Law.

The communication-computation ratio, \(\CommunicationComputeRatio= \CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount)/(\IronLawComputeTime/\DeviceCount)\), compares communication time with ideally partitioned compute time for fixed global work. It is the time consequence of communication intensity \(\CommunicationIntensity\) (equation 1.1), the bytes moved per operation of the workload. When transfers run at link bandwidth \(\LinkBandwidth\) and each device computes at sustained rate \(\PeakRate\EfficiencyHw\), \(\CommunicationComputeRatio\approx \CommunicationIntensity \times \PeakRate\EfficiencyHw/\LinkBandwidth\), so the same workload becomes more communication-bound on faster accelerators or slower links. This ratio identifies the pressure to overlap communication; the complete step time also depends on how much exchange is hidden and how much coordination remains exposed.

↩︎ The \(\MessageStartupLatency\)-\(\LinkBandwidth\) message model, with startup latency \(\MessageStartupLatency\) and sustained bandwidth \(\LinkBandwidth\), is established in The \MessageStartupLatency-\LinkBandwidth performance model.

↪ The communication-computation ratio, evaluated for representative data-parallel and point-to-point transfers and related to communication intensity, is developed in The communication-computation ratio.

Two regimes follow from this ratio. When \(\IronLawComputeTime/\DeviceCount\gg \CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount)\), the cluster is compute-bound, and communication is small relative to useful computation, provided coordination and input delivery do not introduce another limit. Large-batch training of a dense convolutional model such as ResNet commonly sits here. When \(\CommunicationComputeRatio\gtrsim 1\), \(\CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount)\) matches or exceeds \(\IronLawComputeTime/\DeviceCount\) and the cluster is communication-bound. Insufficient overlap then leaves substantial time waiting for gradients or activations. Large language models (LLMs) with billions of parameters to synchronize, and Deep Learning Recommendation Model (DLRM)-style workloads whose embedding exchanges saturate the network, frequently land in this regime. These are typical operating points rather than fixed labels. Batch size, partitioning, and placement can move the same workload across the boundary, so the ratio must be measured for the configuration at hand. Where a workload lands also depends on how its training loop is cut across devices, the choice each parallelism strategy makes.

↪ The optimizations that help, and the ones that are wasted, for a compute-bound, communication-bound, or coordination-bound fleet are collected for reference in The bottleneck diagnostic table.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A research team is training a 3B-parameter transformer that fits comfortably in one H100 GPU’s 80 GB of memory, but projected single-GPU training would take 18 months to reach target loss. The dataset is 3 TB and fits on local NVMe storage. Which signal makes distributed training necessary in this scenario?

    1. Dataset exceeding GPU HBM capacity, which automatically mandates tensor parallelism regardless of execution time
    2. The presence of NVLink inside the node, which makes multi-machine scaling mandatory by default
    3. Unacceptable wall-clock training duration, even though single-device memory capacity and local storage are sufficient
    4. Fault tolerance limitations, because single-GPU training cannot survive hardware restarts

  2. A team doubles a data-parallel cluster from 64 to 128 GPUs and observes that total step time drops by only 12 percent even though per-worker compute time halves. Using the distributed step-time equation \(T_{\text{step}}(N) = T_{\text{compute}}/N + T_{\text{comm}}(N) + T_{\text{sync}}(N) - T_{\text{overlap}}\), explain what the observation establishes and what measurement would distinguish possible causes.

  3. True or False: A cluster of 1,024 GPUs, each with a 50,000-hour mean time to failure, should expect a GPU failure roughly every two days if failures are independent, even though any single GPU fails only about once every five to six years.

  4. The dimensionless ratio \(\rho = T_{\text{comm}}(N) / (T_{\text{compute}}/N)\), which screens communication demand relative to per-worker compute time, is defined as the ____.

See Answers →

6.2 The Parallelism Design Space

Once a model or its training time outgrows a single GPU, the training loop offers three nested axes to split across devices: the examples in a batch, the layers of the model, and the matrix operations inside each layer. Data parallelism assigns different examples to model replicas; pipeline parallelism assigns successive layer groups to different devices; tensor parallelism splits the operations inside a layer. Because every split adds inter-device communication and synchronization overhead on top of single-machine compute and memory limits (The D·A·M Taxonomy), profiling and design must treat these three strategies as loop transformations applied by a cluster-level compiler.

In compiler terms, data parallelism acts like a parallel for-loop that assigns different batch indices to devices running the same model. Tensor parallelism resembles vectorization, or single instruction, multiple data (SIMD), by partitioning the work inside matrix operations. This is an analogy for the work split rather than for a shared instruction stream or register file, since the devices still exchange tensor shards over an interconnect. Pipeline parallelism instead resembles instruction pipelining, assigning successive layer groups to stages so that overlapping microbatches keep those stages active, subject to dependency delays and load balance.

Whichever loop a strategy unrolls, distributed training1 spreads the workload across machines that must coordinate to train a single model. Coordination here means keeping every model shard or replica on a compatible training step. Basic barriers can keep a small research run ordered, but long-running training jobs also need timeout, checkpoint, and recovery mechanisms so one failed worker does not waste days of compute.

1 Distributed training: Google’s DistBelief (2012) was an early framework for training neural networks across thousands of machines, but its parameter server architecture created bandwidth bottlenecks at central nodes. This limitation drove the shift to decentralized AllReduce patterns in successors such as Horovod and PyTorch DistributedDataParallel (DDP), where each data-parallel worker sends and receives about \(2(\DeviceCount-1)/\DeviceCount\) times the gradient size per step rather than concentrating traffic at a single server (Dean et al. 2012; Sergeev and Balso 2018; Li et al. 2020; Patarasuk and Yuan 2009).

Dean, Jeffrey, Greg Corrado, Rajat Monga, Kai Chen, Matthieu Devin, Quoc V. Le, Mark Z. Mao, et al. 2012. “Large Scale Distributed Deep Networks.” In Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS), edited by Peter L. Bartlett, Fernando C. N. Pereira, Christopher J. C. Burges, Léon Bottou, and Kilian Q. Weinberger, vol. 25. Curran Associates.
Sergeev, Alexander, and Mike Del Balso. 2018. “Horovod: Fast and Easy Distributed Deep Learning in TensorFlow.” CoRR abs/1802.05799.
Li, Shen, Yanli Zhao, Rohan Varma, Omkar Salpekar, Pieter Noordhuis, Teng Li, Adam Paszke, et al. 2020. “PyTorch Distributed: Experiences on Accelerating Data Parallel Training.” Proceedings of the VLDB Endowment 13 (12): 3005–18. https://doi.org/10.14778/3415478.3415530.
Patarasuk, Pitch, and Xin Yuan. 2009. “Bandwidth Optimal All-Reduce Algorithms for Clusters of Workstations.” Journal of Parallel and Distributed Computing 69 (2): 117–24. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jpdc.2008.09.002.

↪ Checkpointing and recovery for long-running training jobs are developed in Fault Tolerance.

2 NVLink: NVIDIA’s point-to-point GPU interconnect delivers 300 GB/s–450 GB/s per-direction bandwidth, roughly 12×–18× InfiniBand HDR per port. This bandwidth gap is why tensor parallelism, which requires AllReduce on every layer, favors the fast NVLink domain, while pipeline and data parallelism can tolerate slower links when their traffic budgets fit. NVLink Switch systems can extend that fast domain beyond one server.

Mapping those three loop transformations onto hardware follows the cluster’s interconnect hierarchy. Within a single node, multiple GPUs coordinate across separate device memories over high-bandwidth NVLink or PCIe links,2 with collective libraries such as NCCL scheduling the transfers.

Crossing node boundaries adds slower inter-node network links, fault-tolerance exposure, and cluster orchestration, so single-GPU kernel and memory efficiency must be tuned first rather than multiplied as waste across the fleet. Matching the three loop splits to these intra-node and inter-node tiers decomposes the design space onto a three-dimensional grid, \(\DeviceCount= \DataParallelDegree \times \PipelineParallelDegree \times \TensorParallelDegree\), that partitions cluster execution across tensor parallelism (\(\TensorParallelDegree\), intra-layer sharding), pipeline parallelism (\(\PipelineParallelDegree\), sequential stage partitioning), and data parallelism (\(\DataParallelDegree\), batch replication). Each cell of the grid is one accelerator coordinate (figure 2).

Under the three-dimensional partitioning of figure 2, called 3D parallelism, the total accelerator allocation scales as the product \(\DeviceCount= \DataParallelDegree \times \PipelineParallelDegree \times \TensorParallelDegree\). The axes represent distinct mathematical partitions, but they are not independent configuration knobs. Tensor and pipeline degrees must divide the model structure, the data-parallel degree and microbatch schedule must produce a viable global batch, every partition must fit memory, and each communication group must map onto an adequate topology and acceptable failure domain. Training systems therefore select a jointly feasible coordinate rather than tuning \(\DataParallelDegree\), \(\PipelineParallelDegree\), and \(\TensorParallelDegree\) in isolation.

6.2.1 Parallelism strategy selection and engineering trade-offs

A parallelism strategy that places frequent, high-volume exchanges on a slow link fails before it leaves the whiteboard, because communication stalls the accelerators it was meant to feed. Choosing a strategy is therefore a constraint satisfaction problem governed by parameter count \((\Parameters)\), batch size \((\BatchSize)\), and interconnect bandwidth. A first feasibility screen compares the traffic each strategy creates against the hardware topology (table 1). In that screen, \(\LayerCount\) denotes layer count, \(\SequenceLength\) sequence length, and \(\HiddenDimension\) hidden width. The table identifies traffic sources rather than complete byte budgets; datatype width, partition degree, and communication frequency enter the worked mechanisms below.

Those bandwidth requirements impose a hard constraint on hardware placement: each parallelism axis must map onto an interconnect tier that can sustain its traffic within the compute budget. A quick back-of-envelope bandwidth feasibility check catches a mismatch before any code is written.

↩︎ The communication domains and bandwidth hierarchy used to evaluate placement are established in Bandwidth hierarchy.

Systems Perspective 6.1: Bandwidth feasibility check
Frequent tensor-parallel collectives must fit the layer-time budget. The test compares the time one layer’s local share of computation takes with the time its collectives take on each candidate link. For the forward pass of one GPT-3 layer split 8 ways, processing one 2,048-token sequence at half of an H100’s dense FP16 peak:

  • Local compute per layer: about 1.9 ms
  • Tensor-parallel transfer per layer over NVLink: about 0.4 ms
  • The same transfer over one NDR InfiniBand port: about 3.5 ms

On NVLink the exchange is a fraction of the layer’s compute; across InfiniBand it would take longer than the compute it serves. The amount hidden depends on independent work available in the execution schedule. NVLink is one suitable fast domain, but the test uses message bytes, exposed latency, and sustained link rate rather than an interconnect brand or chassis boundary. Cross-rack placement may favor pipeline or data parallelism when the available fabric cannot meet the tensor-parallel budget; their activation and gradient traffic must also be checked.

The communication costs in table 1 already narrow the choice before any mechanism has been developed, because a strategy is viable only when its traffic fits the tier that must carry it. What each formula implies in practice depends on the mechanics of data, tensor, and pipeline parallelism developed over the rest of the chapter, and section 6.8 turns the same constraints into a decision procedure once those mechanics are in place.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Framing distributed parallelism strategies as compiler loop transformations across the training loop’s iterators (batches, layers, and operations), which distributed strategy corresponds to vectorization (SIMD) of inner matrix multiplications?

    1. Tensor parallelism, which partitions inner matrix-multiplication operations across devices and exchanges partial results over the interconnect
    2. Data parallelism, which unrolls the outer batch loop across devices so each rank processes independent data indices
    3. Pipeline parallelism, which stages sequential layer operations across devices analogous to CPU instruction pipelining
    4. Model replication, which duplicates the entire forward and backward execution redundantly across machines

  2. In the 3D parallelism cube, where the device count equals the product of the data-, pipeline-, and tensor-parallel degrees, how do the three scaling axes interact to allocate cluster accelerators for large models?

    1. The three axes are mutually exclusive; choosing data parallelism precludes using tensor or pipeline parallelism in the same cluster
    2. Tensor and pipeline parallelism can combine within a node, but data parallelism can only be used if model parallelism is disabled
    3. The total accelerator count is the sum of the three degrees, with each parallelism type requiring a disjoint node pool
    4. The axes compose multiplicatively, so one layout can split layers (TP), stage the layer stack (PP), and replicate the pipeline across data shards (DP), and its device count is the product of the three degrees

  3. True or False: If a neural network’s parameters exceed a single GPU’s memory capacity, tensor parallelism can be scaled across standard commodity Ethernet switches between server racks, provided the global batch size is made large enough.

  4. For one GPT-3 layer split eight ways, the chapter’s bandwidth feasibility check finds about 1.9 ms of local compute, about 0.4 ms of tensor-parallel transfer over NVLink, and about 3.5 ms for the same transfer over one NDR InfiniBand port. Explain what these numbers imply for where the tensor-parallel group should be placed and what a planner must still check before accepting that placement.

6.3 Data Parallelism

When a model fits on one accelerator but training takes weeks, replicating its computation lets several workers process examples concurrently. The initial data-parallel baseline gives each worker the same model state and a local portion of one global minibatch. Combining those local contributions before the optimizer update preserves the intended global-batch estimator; the sampling and weighting conditions below explain when that equivalence holds.

Definition 6.2: Data parallelism

Data parallelism distributes training examples across workers that execute the model and combine their contributions to its optimization updates.

  1. Significance: With \(\DeviceCount\) workers each processing a local batch of \(\LocalBatchSize\) examples once per update, the global batch size is \(\DeviceCount\times \LocalBatchSize\). At fixed local work, 1,024 workers offer an ideal 1,024\(\times\) throughput increase before exposed overhead. A 1B-parameter model with BF16 gradients contributes a 2 GB gradient payload; ring AllReduce moves \(2(\DeviceCount-1)/\DeviceCount\) times that payload per worker. Realized speedup depends on local compute, collective service, overlap, and load balance.
  2. Distinction: The replicated synchronous baseline keeps a full model copy on each worker and aggregates gradients before each optimizer update. Model parallelism instead partitions the model’s computation. Sharded data parallelism, developed below, retains data-parallel computation while distributing training-state storage.
  3. Common pitfall: Data parallelism does not scale indefinitely with worker count. Holding the local batch \(\LocalBatchSize\) fixed while adding workers grows the global batch. Beyond the workload-dependent critical batch size, additional examples yield diminishing reductions in the steps needed to reach target quality (Shallue et al. 2019). The crossover developed in section 6.4.6 therefore limits the statistical benefit even when gradient variance continues to decrease.
He, Kaiming, Xiangyu Zhang, Shaoqing Ren, and Jian Sun. 2016. “Deep Residual Learning for Image Recognition.” 2016 IEEE Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (CVPR), 770–78. https://doi.org/10.1109/cvpr.2016.90.

Data parallelism applies when examples can be processed independently, as in training a ResNet model (He et al. 2016) on ImageNet. Each replica computes its local loss gradient, and the workers combine those contributions to reproduce the intended global-batch gradient. The averaging rule makes the sampling and weighting assumptions explicit.

Consider a model with parameters \(\ModelParameters\) training on a dataset \(\TrainingDataset\). The loss function for a single data point \(\ModelInput_i\) is \(\LossFunction(\ModelParameters, \ModelInput_i)\). In standard stochastic gradient descent (SGD) with batch size \(\BatchSize\), the gradient update for a minibatch is: \[ \MinibatchGradient= \frac{1}{\BatchSize} \sum_{i=1}^\BatchSize\nabla_{\ModelParameters} \LossFunction(\ModelParameters, \ModelInput_i) \]

In data parallelism with \(\DeviceCount\) devices, each device \(\WorkerIndex\) computes gradients on its own minibatch \(\MiniBatchSet_k\): \[ \MinibatchGradient_k = \frac{1}{|\MiniBatchSet_k|} \sum_{\ModelInput_i \in \MiniBatchSet_k} \nabla_{\ModelParameters} \LossFunction(\ModelParameters, \ModelInput_i) \]

When all workers use the same local batch size, the global update averages these local gradients: \[ \GlobalBatchGradient= \frac{1}{\DeviceCount} \sum_{k=1}^\DeviceCount\MinibatchGradient_k \]

Under that equal-batch assumption, the averaging is mathematically equivalent to computing the gradient on the combined batch \(\CombinedBatch= \bigcup_{k=1}^\DeviceCount \MiniBatchSet_k\): \[ \GlobalBatchGradient= \frac{1}{|\CombinedBatch|} \sum_{\ModelInput_i \in \CombinedBatch} \nabla_{\ModelParameters} \LossFunction(\ModelParameters, \ModelInput_i) \]

For unequal local batch sizes, the combined-batch gradient is the weighted average \(\GlobalBatchGradient= (1/|\CombinedBatch|)\sum_k |\MiniBatchSet_k|\MinibatchGradient_k\). Data parallelism therefore maintains the statistical properties of SGD training, because sampling the intended global batch across devices, computing local gradient sums independently, and normalizing by the aggregate denominator reproduces its estimator. The reduction step uses an AllReduce over local gradient sums and valid-item counts.

Three batch quantities recur from here on, and the chapter keeps them apart. A worker’s local batch \(\LocalBatchSize\) is the set of examples it processes for one update, playing the role of \(\MiniBatchSet_k\) above. The global batch \(\GlobalBatchSize= \DeviceCount\times \LocalBatchSize\) is the set the update averages over. Pipeline parallelism later splits a batch into \(\MicrobatchCount\) microbatches of size \(\MicrobatchSize\) that flow through stages one after another; the word microbatch is reserved for those pipeline slices.

↪ The ring and tree AllReduce cost models that set the price of this synchronization are developed in AllReduce.

Checkpoint 6.1: Data parallelism mechanics

Verify your understanding of how data parallelism distributes work:

The method parallels gradient accumulation, where a device accumulates contributions from multiple forward and backward passes before one optimizer update. Both techniques use gradient addition and the intended loss denominator to process a larger effective batch. Distribution adds communication, partial failures, and coordination to that arithmetic. The implementation must preserve the update while accounting for those costs.

6.3.1 Data parallelism implementation

The implementation details matter because the SGD equivalence only holds when each phase preserves the intended aggregate sampling and weighting, complete local gradient contributions, and a single synchronized update. The concrete workflow follows the dataflow from assigning local batches to synchronizing the computed gradients. The five-stage execution pipeline in figure 3 follows four GPU swimlanes. Identical model replicas ingest disjoint batch shards and compute local gradients (\(\MinibatchGradient_0\)–\(\MinibatchGradient_3\)). They then synchronize through a ring AllReduce collective before applying uniform parameter updates.

The gradient synchronization stage of figure 3 (stage 4) creates a dependency, because each rank must receive the completed reduction before applying its optimizer update. The exposed wait can reflect bandwidth, collective startup, or a late contribution. Measuring that wait determines whether synchronization limits scaling; the dependency alone does not identify the binding resource.

Dataset splitting

Data splitting is the first place where the SGD equivalence can fail, but uniqueness and equal-sized shards are sufficient conditions rather than the invariant itself. The aggregate examples or tokens, their sampling probabilities, and their gradient weights must reproduce the intended global-batch estimator. Uniform sampling without replacement can assign disjoint shards. Sampling with replacement can legitimately repeat examples. Variable-length batches can assign unequal token counts if the reduction weights each worker by its valid-example or valid-token denominator. An unweighted mean of unequal local means instead overweights workers with smaller denominators.

The sampler is therefore an integral component of the distributed training system rather than merely an input-loader convenience. A distributed sampler coordinates workers by rank and can deterministically partition or draw indices from a seeded sampling stream. The 1.28 million training images of ImageNet-1k do not divide evenly across 32 GPUs. A sampler that pads by repeating examples gives each GPU 40,037 examples per epoch and adds 17 repeats across the job. The loss normalization or sample weights must account for those repeats when exact epoch-level weighting matters. A recorded seed and deterministic index stream make sample assignment repeatable. Bitwise run reproducibility additionally depends on kernel determinism, collective reduction order, numerical precision, and checkpointed random-number-generator state.

Compute-phase forward and backward passes

For a replicated model whose local forward and backward operations require no cross-worker state, the computation phase is embarrassingly parallel. Each GPU executes the model on its local batch; synchronized normalization or additional model partitioning can introduce communication within that phase. To isolate the activation term, consider an unpartitioned 175B reference model at a local batch of 4 sequences and sequence length 2048. This is a hypothetical memory budget, not a feasible one-GPU training configuration, because weights and optimizer state must fit as well. Without optimization, storing these activations for backpropagation would consume roughly 1.1 TB of HBM, an order of magnitude beyond the capacity of even an H100 GPU. Activation checkpointing recomputes selected activations during the backward pass rather than retaining them, reducing this example’s footprint to ~19.3 GB at the cost of extra computation.

The capacity test must include activations rather than stopping at bytes per parameter. Numeric Precision and Training Memory tabulates the 16-byte-per-parameter training state from which this test starts. At the point of peak residency, \[ \TotalMemoryFootprint = \WeightMemory + \GradientMemory + \OptimizerStateMemory + \ActivationMemory, \] with communication buffers and temporary workspaces consuming additional headroom. Sharding reduces the first three terms according to its stage, whereas sequence length, local batch size, and pipeline scheduling govern \(\ActivationMemory\). Activation checkpointing reduces that last term by retaining only selected boundaries and recomputing the omitted forward operators during backpropagation (Narayanan et al. 2021). Full recomputation re-executes about one extra forward pass per step. Because a backward pass costs roughly two forward passes, a step that needed three forward-pass equivalents of arithmetic now needs four, about a third more (Chen et al. 2016). Selective recomputation re-executes only operators whose activations are large but cheap to produce, such as the attention softmax and dropout, and keeps most of the memory saving for a few percent of extra arithmetic (Korthikanti et al. 2023). Recomputed operations raise hardware FLOPs utilization (HFU) but not model FLOPs utilization (MFU), which credits only the model’s own forward and backward work. A plan that buys feasibility with full recomputation therefore pays for it in useful throughput. The memory saved is purchased with additional FLOPs and a longer critical path. A feasible parallelism plan must budget both effects.

Narayanan, Deepak, Mohammad Shoeybi, Jared Casper, Patrick LeGresley, Mostofa Patwary, Vijay Korthikanti, Dmitri Vainbrand, et al. 2021. “Efficient Large-Scale Language Model Training on GPU Clusters Using Megatron-LM.” Proceedings of the International Conference for High Performance Computing, Networking, Storage and Analysis, 1–15. https://doi.org/10.1145/3458817.3476209.
Chen, Tianqi, Bing Xu, Chiyuan Zhang, and Carlos Guestrin. 2016. “Training Deep Nets with Sublinear Memory Cost.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1604.06174.

As the backward pass traverses the computation graph in reverse, it produces gradients for the parameters held by each replica or shard. Under pure data parallelism, a full 175B FP16 replica would imply a 350 GB gradient tensor per worker, which exceeds single-accelerator memory budgets. Large runs therefore combine data parallelism with sharding, tensor parallelism, or pipeline parallelism. Each local gradient contributes to the intended global-batch estimator. Aggregation can begin as gradient tensors become ready, overlapping later backward computation, but the optimizer must wait for the completed contributions it consumes. This readiness schedule connects local computation to collective communication.

Gradient synchronization

Gradient synchronization combines local contributions into the synchronous update. Each replica must receive the aggregate before applying that update. In a centralized parameter-server design with fixed per-worker payload and update frequency, the server receives \(\mathcal{O}(\DeviceCount)\) incoming traffic. That concentration becomes a bottleneck when demand exceeds the server tier’s service capacity; sharding the server state or changing the communication pattern can relieve it.

The replicated synchronous baseline distributes aggregation through collective communication. AllReduce lets each worker contribute its local gradient tensor and receive the same reduced result, which the training loop normalizes to obtain the global average. In sharded variants such as ZeRO and FSDP, ReduceScatter and AllGather change which result each worker retains, leaving each worker only its own shard of the reduced gradient and reconstructing full parameters on demand. Small messages emphasize startup latency; large messages emphasize sustained bandwidth. Centralized aggregation remains another design choice, particularly for the sparse workloads developed later.

↩︎ The result contracts of AllReduce, ReduceScatter, and AllGather, and the decomposition of AllReduce into a ReduceScatter followed by an AllGather, are established in The six core primitives.

At the Distributed Execution row of the fleet stack, the loop specifies an AllReduce contract while its implementation determines the cost. For a large worker group, ring AllReduce transfers approximately \(2\GradientPayload\) bytes per worker for an \(\GradientPayload\)-byte gradient payload. Collective Communication derived this approximate \(2\GradientPayload\) volume and compared algorithms whose startup costs, topology, and contention differ. That chapter also examined how libraries such as NCCL schedule exchanges within and across communication domains.

When a measured collective deviates from these estimates, the diagnosis compares the trace against what each candidate algorithm predicts on the same hardware. A typical comparison sets a flat ring against a hierarchical AllReduce, which reduces within each NVLink node before crossing the inter-node fabric. A hypothetical trace on an A100 cluster with HDR InfiniBand shows the comparison at work. An AllReduce of a 3 GB gradient tensor across 128 nodes (1,024 GPUs) takes 100 ms, against a flat-ring estimate of 239.8 ms and an idealized hierarchical estimate of 48.5 ms. The observation rules out the flat schedule, and the residual above the hierarchical ideal must come from lower achieved rates, startup, or a late participant. Switch counters showing inter-node links at 60 percent utilization do not settle the question, because an aggregate can hide one saturated port during the exposed interval. Per-port and per-channel traces must locate the bottleneck before any topology change.

↩︎ Hierarchical AllReduce and its cost relative to a flat ring are established in Hierarchical AllReduce.

Gibiansky, A. 2017. Bringing HPC Techniques to Deep Learning. Baidu Research Technical Blog.

Across the wider design space, figure 4 compares three synchronization topologies. The bandwidth-optimal ring AllReduce circulates chunks along neighbor links (\(2(\DeviceCount-1)/\DeviceCount\) volume). The centralized parameter server is constrained by network interface card (NIC) saturation at the server node. The dense mesh of logical AllToAll peer traffic requires \(\DeviceCount(\DeviceCount-1)\) messages. In dense synchronous data-parallel settings, ring AllReduce avoids a single reducer bottleneck by distributing traffic evenly across participating links, as Baidu’s implementation illustrates (Gibiansky 2017).

The panels separate an aggregation algorithm, a centralized architecture, and a collective traffic pattern. They do not provide interchangeable implementations of the same operation. Only the first two aggregate gradients; the AllToAll panel previews the exchange that sparse embedding tables and mixture-of-experts layers need, in which every worker sends different data to every other worker (section 6.5.5). Libraries choose algorithms and routes for the requested collective on the available fabric; a logical complete graph does not guarantee one-round physical delivery.

Parameter updating

Parameter updating closes the replicated synchronous contract. After the gradient AllReduce (Patarasuk and Yuan 2009), every worker holds the same global gradient and applies it to its own replica with SGD, momentum, or Adam, so the update stays decentralized without losing equivalence to single-device training. Identical gradients alone are insufficient, because optimizer moments, learning rates, and step counts must also match across replicas. Frameworks automate the cycle of splitting data, computing gradients, synchronizing, and updating, but they cannot remove the ordering constraint that keeps the replicas coherent: every worker must finish aggregating the current step’s gradient before any replica computes from the updated weights.

Synchronization models

Whether every worker must wait for that barrier on every step defines the job’s synchronization model. The baseline is bulk synchronous parallel (BSP), the superstep-and-barrier model just described, in which workers compute from one parameter version, combine their gradient contributions, and complete the update before computing from the next version. This is a dependency order, not a requirement that every device update at the same wall-clock instant. Relaxing it changes the optimization process as well as the waiting time.

↩︎ Bulk synchronous parallel execution and its barrier cost are established in How ML Networking Inverts Data-Center Assumptions.

BSP provides synchronous update semantics, in which every worker computes from the same parameter version and reaches the next update boundary together. With the same global batch and reduction, this matches the corresponding synchronous single-device update mathematically. It does not by itself guarantee bitwise reproducibility. Floating-point reduction order, nondeterministic kernels, data-order state, and failure recovery can still change the result. The synchronization cost is that the slowest worker determines iteration time. A worker that keeps producing correct results but runs markedly slower than its peers is a straggler, and under BSP every straggler delays the entire step.

Stale synchronous parallel (SSP) relaxes this constraint by allowing workers to proceed up to \(\StalenessBound\) iterations ahead of the slowest worker before blocking. This bounds staleness while reducing synchronization delays. SSP requires careful learning rate tuning since workers compute gradients on slightly different parameter versions. The bounded staleness guarantee provides a middle ground between BSP’s strong consistency and fully asynchronous approaches (Ho et al. 2013).

Asynchronous parallel (ASP) execution removes the global update barrier, allowing a worker’s contribution to be applied while others continue computing; asynchronous SGD is the optimizer run under that protocol. Less barrier waiting can improve update throughput, but communication and shared-state contention remain. Gradients computed from older parameter versions introduce staleness that can affect convergence. Section 6.4.4 develops the objective and delay assumptions needed to evaluate that trade-off. The three models lie on one spectrum, from the strong consistency of BSP to the barrier-free updates of ASP (table 2).

Execution traces for four workers with one straggler make the differences visible (figure 5). Under BSP, global barriers force the faster workers into idle wait blocks while straggler W3 completes. Under SSP, bounded slack (\(\StalenessBound=1\)) lets them run ahead and throttles the drift with brief pauses. Under ASP, asynchronous updates remove the global barrier without imposing a finite staleness bound, although communication and resource contention can still leave workers waiting.

Barrier semantics and failure modes

An optimizer update that consumes an AllReduce result must wait for every required contribution. Workers can execute independent computation while a collective is in flight, but they must issue compatible collectives in the same group order and wait for completion before consuming the result. This coupling creates failure modes absent from single-device training.

A worker failure can leave peers waiting for a contribution that will never arrive. The runtime may report a communication error, or the operation may remain blocked until a timeout detects the failure. Watchdogs bound that wait so the job can enter recovery rather than retain an unusable allocation indefinitely.

One missing worker stalls the entire AllReduce barrier.

Collective mismatches occur when workers issue incompatible operations, tensor shapes, or ordering within a communication group. Conditional computation can create such a mismatch if some workers omit an expected reduction. Variable sequence lengths or different expert routes do not inherently violate the protocol; the runtime must construct a compatible communication schedule for those varying local computations.

Stragglers extend an iteration when their late work lies on its critical path. Thermal throttling, network congestion, or OS jitter can delay a required contribution even when other workers are ready. At 1,000 GPUs with a 1 percent straggler probability per GPU per step, the expected count is 10 stragglers per step, but the number that matters under BSP is the chance of a step with none at all. That chance is the per-GPU on-time probability raised to the GPU count, about 0.004 percent. Almost every step therefore waits for at least one slow worker, which is why the slowest worker, not the typical one, sets step time. The resulting exposed wait depends on how late that contribution arrives and what independent work remains. Deployed systems bound these waits with timeouts and heartbeat monitoring, and elastic training lets a job continue with a resized worker group after a failure.

↪ Heartbeat and collective-timeout failure detection, and the trade-off between restarting healthy workers and leaving peers blocked, are developed in Failure detection mechanisms.

6.3.2 Data parallelism trade-offs

Data parallelism is the simplest way to add accelerators because it leaves each replica’s model computation intact, but adding workers eventually hits either a communication wall or a statistical wall (Ben-Nun and Hoefler 2019). Near-linear throughput scaling requires local compute efficiency, balanced input delivery, and small exposed synchronization costs. Which wall arrives first depends on whether the global batch stays fixed or grows as workers are added. Holding the global batch fixed while adding workers is strong scaling: each worker’s local batch shrinks, so communication must hide behind less computation. Holding the local batch fixed so that the global batch grows with \(\DeviceCount\) is weak scaling: per-worker computation stays constant, but each update averages more examples, which eventually stops reducing the number of updates needed. The rest of the chapter refers to the two regimes by these names.

Ben-Nun, Tal, and Torsten Hoefler. 2019. “Demystifying Parallel and Distributed Deep Learning: An in-Depth Concurrency Analysis.” ACM Computing Surveys 52 (4): 1–43. https://doi.org/10.1145/3320060.

Weak scaling pays off when the larger global batch still reaches the target accuracy. Goyal et al. (2017) trained ResNet-50 on ImageNet in about one hour on 256 GPUs at a global batch of 8,192 images; the learning-rate rules that let that batch reach the target accuracy are developed in section 6.4.5. Replicated data parallelism retains the model’s local operators, and frameworks can trigger gradient reductions as backward computation produces them. Each replica executes a complete forward and backward pass, avoiding the layer-stage fill and drain bubbles of pipeline parallelism. Input stalls, imbalance, and exposed reductions can still leave devices idle.

These advantages hold only until replication runs into memory capacity, communication bandwidth, or statistical diminishing returns. The memory wall binds first on large models because a replicated worker holds every parameter, gradient, and optimizer state, demanding more than 1 TB per replica for the 175B-parameter example before activations. Even when the state fits, communication limits useful scaling once its exposed cost grows relative to local work. As \(\DeviceCount\) increases, the ideal ring bandwidth term \(\frac{2(\DeviceCount-1)}{\DeviceCount} \frac{\GradientPayload}{\LinkBandwidth}\) approaches a constant for a fixed gradient payload \(\GradientPayload\) and link bandwidth \(\LinkBandwidth\), while startup latency, topology, and shrinking local computation worsen the balance. Statistical efficiency then sets the ceiling under weak scaling, because growing \(\GlobalBatchSize= \DeviceCount\times \LocalBatchSize\) with \(\DeviceCount\) eventually yields diminishing reductions in update count. Strong scaling avoids that batch-size ceiling only by shrinking each worker’s local batch, which exposes the communication trade-off sooner.

Napkin Math 6.1: GPT-2 data parallel scaling: Single node
A GPT-2 scaling scenario makes the efficiency loss concrete by holding the model fixed and changing only the communication domain. GPT-2 stands in for the 175B reference here because it fits on one GPU, so pure replicated data parallelism applies and communication is the only variable. The first case keeps all eight GPUs inside a single NVLink node.

Single-GPU baseline

  • Batch size: 16 (with gradient checkpointing, fits in 32 GB)
  • Time per step: 1.8 s
  • Baseline time for 50K single-GPU steps: 25 hours

The following durations hold the total sample budget fixed, so larger global batches require fewer optimizer updates. They assume those samples attain the same quality; the calculation establishes throughput scaling, not convergence equivalence.

8 GPUs: single node with NVLink

  • Per-GPU batch: 16, global batch: 128
  • Gradient synchronization: 5.2 GB @ 450 GB/s (NVLink, per direction) \(\approx\) 11.7 ms

Performance results:

  • Compute: 1800 ms per step
  • Communication: 11.7 ms per step
  • Total: 1811.7 ms per step
  • Speedup (throughput): 8×
  • Parallel efficiency: 99.4 percent

Training time: 25 hours ÷ 8 = 3.1 hours

Inside the node, NVLink keeps gradient exchange small relative to compute, so efficiency stays near 99.4 percent. The picture inverts once the same model must synchronize across nodes over a commodity network.

Napkin Math 6.2: GPT-2 data parallel scaling: Commodity scale-out

The second case scales the same GPT-2 run across multiple nodes, replacing the intra-node NVLink hop with inter-node Ethernet for the bulk of the AllReduce. The 10 Gb Ethernet link models a commodity cloud tier without a dedicated training fabric, far slower than the InfiniBand of the reference cluster, which makes the cost of leaving the node easy to see.

Commodity network configuration: 32 GPUs across 4 nodes

  • Per-GPU batch: 16, global batch: 512
  • Intra-node communication: 11.7 ms (NVLink)
  • Inter-node communication: 5.8 GB @ 1.25 GB/s (10GbE) \(\approx\) 4650 ms

Performance results:

  • Compute: 1800 ms (27.9 percent of time)
  • Communication: 4661.7 ms (72.1 percent of time), so communication dominates and becomes the bottleneck.
  • Total: 6461.7 ms per step
  • Speedup (throughput): 8.9× faster → 2.8 hours
  • Parallel efficiency: 27.9 percent

The scale-out run bought its larger global batch with four nodes on a slow network. A single node can reach the same global batch another way, by running several local microsteps and accumulating their gradients before one AllReduce. The comparison below is therefore between two placements, one node with accumulation against four nodes without it. Accumulation’s job is to keep the global batch equal, while the saving comes from never leaving the NVLink domain.

Napkin Math 6.3: Gradient accumulation speedup
Problem: A GPT-2 run on a commodity 10G network is communication-bound at 32 GPUs across 4 nodes, costing $359 for a fixed number of samples. Can a single 8-GPU node that uses gradient accumulation to match that global batch process the same samples for less?

Math:

  1. Effective batch size: 8 GPUs \(\times\) batch 16 \(\times\) 4 accumulation steps = 512.
  2. Communication overhead: With 4-step accumulation, the job AllReduces once every 4 steps (overhead = 11.7 ms / (4 \(\times\) 1800 ms) \(\approx\) \(0.162\%\)).
  3. Training duration: Total time is 3.1 hours.
  4. Scale-out baseline cost: 4 nodes \(\times\) 2.8 hours \(\times\) $32/hr = $359.
  5. Accumulation cost: 3.1 hours \(\times\) $32/hr = $100.

Systems insight: The one-node plan saves $259 (72.1 percent). Almost all of that saving comes from placement, because the four-node run spent most of each step on the slow inter-node link and the single node never uses it. Gradient accumulation is what makes the single node a fair substitute, because it reproduces the four-node global batch. Its own contribution to speed is small, since the same node already ran at 99.4 percent parallel efficiency without accumulation. Accumulation on a multi-node job still requires inter-node synchronization once per window.

In this GPT-2 scenario, changing the communication domain drops efficiency from 99.4 percent to 27.9 percent. Accumulation can preserve the effective batch while avoiding the slow scale-out link, at the cost of additional local computation between updates. It leaves each worker’s replicated model state unchanged, however; reducing that memory burden requires sharding.

6.3.3 Memory-efficient data parallelism with ZeRO and FSDP

Replicated data parallelism stores an identical copy of weights, gradients, and optimizer state on every GPU, so a 7B-parameter model demands 112 GB on each device and overflows an 80 GB accelerator before a single activation is allocated. The Zero Redundancy Optimizer (ZeRO) (Rajbhandari et al. 2020) and PyTorch’s Fully Sharded Data Parallel (FSDP) (Zhao et al. 2023) remove that duplication by sharding training state across workers while keeping each worker’s full forward and backward computation.3 Sharding can make a model fit in memory without splitting its layer computations across devices.

Rajbhandari, Samyam, Jeff Rasley, Olatunji Ruwase, and Yuxiong He. 2020. “ZeRO: Memory Optimizations Toward Training Trillion Parameter Models.” SC20: International Conference for High Performance Computing, Networking, Storage and Analysis, 1–16. https://doi.org/10.1109/sc41405.2020.00024.
Zhao, Yanli, Andrew Gu, Rohan Varma, Liang Luo, Chien-Chin Huang, Min Xu, Less Wright, et al. 2023. “PyTorch FSDP: Experiences on Scaling Fully Sharded Data Parallel.” Proceedings of the VLDB Endowment 16 (12): 3848–60. https://doi.org/10.14778/3611540.3611569.

3 ZeRO (Zero Redundancy Optimizer): Introduced by Microsoft researchers in 2019 and released in the DeepSpeed library (Rasley et al. 2020), ZeRO’s stages progressively partition optimizer states, gradients, and parameters. FSDP draws on the fully sharded approach associated with ZeRO’s deepest stage; the shared partitioning principle does not imply identical implementations or communication schedules.

Rasley, Jeff, Samyam Rajbhandari, Olatunji Ruwase, and Yuxiong He. 2020. “DeepSpeed: System Optimizations Enable Training Deep Learning Models with over 100 Billion Parameters.” Proceedings of the 26th ACM SIGKDD International Conference on Knowledge Discovery & Data Mining, 3505–6. https://doi.org/10.1145/3394486.3406703.
Definition 6.3: Sharded data parallelism

Sharded data parallelism partitions optimizer state, gradients, and at the deepest stage the parameters themselves across data-parallel workers. Each worker processes its own minibatch shard and uses collectives to obtain the parameters and reduced gradients it needs. Full sharding reduces each worker’s persistent state toward \(1/\DeviceCount\) of the replicated training state; activations and temporary buffers still contribute to peak memory.

  1. Significance: Under the mixed-precision Adam convention used here, 16 bytes of state per parameter gives a 7B-parameter model 112 GB of training state, exceeding a 80 GB accelerator before activations. ZeRO Stage 3 across 64 workers reduces persistent state per device to 1.75 GB (0.25 bytes per parameter). The resulting AllGather and ReduceScatter traffic trades memory capacity for communication whose exposed cost depends on the interconnect and available overlap.
  2. Distinction: Unlike vanilla data parallelism, which replicates training state, sharded data parallelism partitions its storage. Unlike model parallelism, it retains each worker’s full forward and backward computation. Fully sharded execution gathers parameters for the current computation; the resharding schedule determines when it releases them.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that sharding is free memory. The on-demand AllGathers place parameter traffic on the critical path of every layer in every step. On slower interconnects or at small per-worker batch sizes, the exposed communication erodes throughput faster than the memory savings help. Capacity is purchased with bandwidth.

The scale of the savings is clearest on a model whose replicated training state only modestly overflows one accelerator, so that the effect of each sharding stage stays visible. The 175B reference returns after this trace when the question becomes whether sharding alone is enough.

Napkin Math 6.4: ZeRO memory savings
Problem: Training a 7B parameter Llama-2 model in mixed precision requires 16 bytes per parameter for weights, gradients, and optimizer state. Does the full training state fit on a single A100-80 GB, and how does ZeRO Stage 3 with 64 GPUs change the per-device memory requirement?

Baseline: Standard DDP (replicated state) Per-Parameter Memory Cost:

  • Weights (FP16): 2 bytes
  • Gradients (FP16): 2 bytes
  • Optimizer state (FP32): 12 bytes (4 master weight + 4 momentum + 4 variance)
  • Total: 16 bytes/parameter

Total Memory for 7B Model:

\[C_{\text{state,total}} = 7 \times 10^9 \times 16 \text{ bytes} \approx 112 \text{ GB}\]

Baseline outcome: Out-of-memory (OOM) on A100-80 GB.

Optimization: ZeRO-3 (fully sharded) With \(\DeviceCount=\) 64 GPUs, state is partitioned:

  • Weights: 2\(/\DeviceCount\) bytes
  • Gradients: 2\(/\DeviceCount\) bytes
  • Optimizer: 12\(/\DeviceCount\) bytes
  • Total: 16\(/\DeviceCount=\) 0.25 bytes/parameter effective storage

Per-GPU Memory:

\[C_{\text{state,ZeRO3}} = \frac{112 \text{ GB}}{64} \approx 1.75 \text{ GB}\]

Result: The persistent state fits, leaving ~78 GB for activations, gathered parameters, temporary buffers, and runtime allocations. Their simultaneous peak determines whether the chosen batch size fits.

ZeRO reaches that result in stages, and the intermediate stages matter because each one buys memory with a different communication pattern. Optimizer sharding alone (ZeRO-1) already brings the replicated footprint of the same model under the accelerator ceiling, because optimizer state is three-quarters of it. Gradient sharding (ZeRO-2) and parameter sharding (ZeRO-3/FSDP) continue toward the fully sharded figure (figure 6). Each stage also changes which collectives the update uses (table 3).

The first step, ZeRO-1, shards optimizer states across GPUs so each GPU stores only \(1/\DeviceCount\) of the Adam optimizer-related state. During backpropagation, each worker still materializes the full 2 bytes/param gradient buffer locally, which is why gradient memory remains unsharded in table 3. Once gradients are ready, however, they need only be reduced to the worker responsible for each parameter shard via a ReduceScatter, and after each worker updates its owned shard, an AllGather broadcasts the updated parameters to restore the full replicas. This two-phase schedule (one gradient ReduceScatter plus one parameter AllGather) matches the table’s \(1\times\) baseline communication volume, whereas running a full gradient AllReduce followed by a parameter AllGather would add 50 percent more traffic. Under the 12-byte convention that counts FP32 master weights, momentum, and variance, sharding the optimizer state reduces cluster-wide storage from \(12\DeviceCount\) bytes/param to \(12\) bytes/param in total.

Building on that owner-update contract, ZeRO-2 eliminates persistent full-model gradient buffers by streaming the ReduceScatter during the backward pass. As each layer or communication bucket finishes its backward computation, workers immediately ReduceScatter that bucket’s gradients and free the slices owned by other ranks, leaving each GPU with only its \(1/\DeviceCount\) persistent shard of reduced gradients. Each worker then updates its \(1/\DeviceCount\) parameter partition using the corresponding optimizer state, and an AllGather restores the updated parameter replicas. In the ideal ring accounting, the gradient ReduceScatter and the parameter AllGather each transfer \(\Parameters(\DeviceCount-1)/\DeviceCount\) elements per worker, for \(2\Parameters(\DeviceCount-1)/\DeviceCount\) across both phases, matching DDP’s byte volume (\(1\times\)) when gradient and parameter communication widths match. Replicated parameters avoid forward parameter gathers, although model operations may still communicate. Under the FP16-gradient convention, persistent gradient storage falls from \(2\DeviceCount\) bytes per parameter across \(\DeviceCount\) workers to \(2\) bytes per parameter in total, or \(2/\DeviceCount\) per GPU, apart from transient bucket buffers.

At the deepest stage, ZeRO-3 and FSDP partition the parameters as well, leaving each GPU with a \(1/\DeviceCount\) persistent slice of all three training states. Because workers no longer hold full parameter replicas between steps, the post-update parameter AllGather disappears and is replaced by two on-demand parameter AllGathers during computation. Each sharded unit gathers its parameters before the forward pass and immediately releases the non-local slices, then gathers them a second time before the backward pass and releases them again once local gradients are computed, and finally runs a ReduceScatter to deliver the completed gradients to each shard’s owner for the local optimizer update. Across four ranks (figure 7) and under matching communication widths, ZeRO-1 (\(\OptimizerStateMemory/4\)) and ZeRO-2 (\(\OptimizerStateMemory/4, \GradientMemory/4\)) each execute two collective phases per step and retain the baseline \(1\times\) communication volume. ZeRO-3 (\(\WeightMemory/4, \GradientMemory/4, \OptimizerStateMemory/4\)) executes three collective phases of one parameter-equivalent payload each (forward AllGather, backward AllGather, and gradient ReduceScatter), which yields \(3/2 = 1.5\times\) the baseline two-phase volume in this accounting.

Full sharding adds parameter-readiness dependencies that replicated DDP avoids. With one sharding unit per layer, the forward pass needs an AllGather to reconstruct parameters (\(\LayerParameterPayload\) bytes). Resharding after forward requires another gather before backward (\(\LayerParameterPayload\) bytes), followed by a ReduceScatter for gradients (\(\LayerParameterPayload\) bytes under equal communication precision). For a model with \(\LayerCount\) layers, this schedule performs about \(3\LayerCount\) collective operations per step. DDP instead AllReduces gradient buckets as they become ready. At large worker counts, the ideal per-worker transfer volumes approach \(3\ParameterPayload\) bytes versus \(2\ParameterPayload\) for DDP. Here \(\ParameterPayload\) is the model’s parameter bytes at the communication precision, not full training state, and it equals \(\GradientPayload\) when parameters and gradients travel at the same width. These volumes do not determine elapsed time alone. While layer \(\LayerIndex\) computes, layer \(\LayerIndex+1\) can prefetch parameters, subject to memory headroom and fabric contention.

The allocation lifetimes in figure 8 separate persistent ownership from the temporary buffers needed to execute one sharding unit.

A model fits only when all simultaneously live allocations fit. The persistent shards therefore establish a floor, while gathered units, saved activations, and prefetch determine how far the execution peak rises above it.

The choice between FSDP and DDP begins with peak memory. If replicated state, activations, and working buffers fit, DDP avoids repeated parameter gathers. When optimizer and gradient storage are the obstacle, ZeRO-2 offers savings while leaving parameters replicated. If parameters themselves do not fit, ZeRO-3/FSDP, offload, or partitioned computation must address that remaining constraint. For a 70B+ model on 80 GB devices, the need for tensor parallelism depends on the gathered layer size, activation budget, and throughput target; parameter count alone does not decide it.

Memory-efficient data parallelism requires careful tuning of sharding strategy (by layer, by transformer block, or flat) and mixed precision settings. Finer sharding reduces per-GPU memory but increases communication frequency, because more AllGather and ReduceScatter operations must execute per training step. Replicated or sharded, a data-parallel design earns its added workers only when they turn into faster progress toward target quality, and scaling efficiency measures that conversion.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A 7B-parameter transformer in mixed precision requires 112 GB of static memory for standard replicated DDP (14 GB for FP16 weights, 14 GB for FP16 gradients, and 84 GB for FP32 Adam master weights, momentum, and variance). Under ZeRO Stage 3 (or FSDP Full Shard) across 64 GPUs, what is the resulting static training memory per GPU?

    1. \(112\text{ GB}\), because ZeRO-3 only reduces dynamic activation memory rather than model state
    2. \(28\text{ GB}\), because only optimizer states are sharded while parameters and gradients remain fully replicated
    3. \(1.75\text{ GB}\), because weights, gradients, and optimizer states are each partitioned equally across all 64 workers
    4. \(0.25\text{ GB}\), because INT4 quantization eliminates floating-point representation entirely

  2. An 8-GPU node can use four-step gradient accumulation to match the effective batch of 32 GPUs connected by 10 Gb/s Ethernet. Explain why the smaller allocation can cost less even when the larger allocation completes the fixed-sample workload sooner.

  3. Order the memory sharding stages of data parallelism from least sharded (highest memory footprint) to deepest sharded (lowest memory footprint):

  1. ZeRO-2 (shards optimizer states and gradients using ReduceScatter)
  2. Standard DDP (full replication of parameters, gradients, and optimizer states)
  3. ZeRO-3 / FSDP (shards optimizer states, gradients, and model parameters with layer-wise AllGather/ReduceScatter)
  4. ZeRO-1 (shards optimizer states only, keeping parameters and gradients replicated)

  1. If a model’s parameters, gradients, optimizer state, activations, and working buffers already fit comfortably within single-GPU memory with high compute utilization under standard DDP, what is the expected throughput impact of enabling ZeRO-3 / FSDP Full Sharding?

    1. Throughput will increase linearly by \(N\times\) because sharding reduces per-GPU gradient memory
    2. Throughput can decrease because FSDP introduces additional AllGather collectives before forward and backward passes and ReduceScatter on the critical path
    3. Throughput will remain exactly identical because NCCL automatically hides all collective communication overhead
    4. Throughput will double because parameter sharding eliminates the backward pass entirely

  2. Why did modern distributed training frameworks replace centralized parameter servers with decentralized AllReduce collective topologies for dense synchronous training?

    1. Parameter servers cannot execute stochastic gradient descent updates with mathematical validity
    2. Parameter servers are restricted to CPU hosts and cannot interface with GPU accelerators
    3. AllReduce eliminates all network traffic by updating weights locally without exchanging data
    4. Centralized parameter servers create an \(\mathcal{O}(N)\) network bottleneck at the server’s network interface, whereas AllReduce distributes communication symmetrically across all workers

  3. In standard PyTorch DistributedDataParallel, each worker uses a ____ to partition dataset indices deterministically and assign rank-specific shards based on process rank and epoch seed.

6.4 Scaling Efficiency and Convergence

Data parallelism and ZeRO sharding show how communication and synchronization enter every training step, yet raw step throughput alone cannot judge a distributed run. When doubling the number of GPUs yields only 1.5\(\times\) speedup for the same work, scaling efficiency is 75 percent, and if the larger global batch also requires more optimizer updates to reach the same loss, wall-clock time to quality degrades further. Evaluating a distributed configuration therefore requires separating two questions: how efficiently added devices execute each step, and how batch growth or stale updates affect the number of steps to convergence. The hardware scaling metrics below apply to every parallelism strategy; the convergence analysis that follows concerns data-parallel batch growth and synchronization slack, establishing a batch-size constraint that tensor and pipeline configurations must also respect when combined with data parallelism.

6.4.1 The mathematics of scaling efficiency

That first question is answered by scaling efficiency, the fleet-level ratio introduced with the Fleet Law in The Fleet Law. If a model trains in time \(\SingleDeviceRunTime\) on one device, ideal (linear) scaling would train it in time \(\SingleDeviceRunTime/\DeviceCount\) on \(\DeviceCount\) devices; communication overhead, pipeline bubbles, and load imbalance widen the gap between that ideal and the realized time \(\MultiDeviceRunTime\). Equation 1 formalizes the ratio:

\[\ScalingEfficiency = \frac{\SingleDeviceRunTime}{\DeviceCount\times \MultiDeviceRunTime} \tag{1}\]

where \(\MultiDeviceRunTime\) is the training time on \(\DeviceCount\) devices. Measured per step of the same work, \(\SingleDeviceRunTime\) is the quantity the Fleet Law writes as \(\IronLawComputeTime\), the single-device compute time that its compute term divides by \(\DeviceCount\). An efficiency of 1.0 means perfect linear scaling; an efficiency of 0.5 means the run achieves only half the ideal speedup.

Definition 6.4: Scaling efficiency

Scaling efficiency \((\ScalingEfficiency)\) is the ratio of actual training throughput to ideal linear throughput when increasing the number of ML compute devices (\(\DeviceCount\)).

  1. Significance: The ratio in equation 1 measures added devices against the specified baseline. At 0.50 efficiency, a 10,000-GPU run delivers the throughput that 5,000 perfectly scaled devices would, so half of the accelerator-hours buy no progress relative to ideal scaling. The equivalence is to an idealized baseline. A real 5,000-GPU run would have its own, typically higher, efficiency, and the loss is spread across every device as waiting rather than concentrated in idle ones.
  2. Distinction: Unlike single-node efficiency, which captures local bottlenecks like \(\Bandwidth\), scaling efficiency measures how performance changes with device count. Communication time \((\CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount))\) and synchronization contribute, but changing local kernel shapes or input delivery can also affect the ratio.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that scaling efficiency is constant. In reality, it depends on both device count and problem size. For a fixed model, the communication-to-compute ratio typically worsens as \(\DeviceCount\) increases (Amdahl’s Law). At a fixed \(\DeviceCount\), a smaller model or local batch leaves less computation per device to hide startup latency and coordination, so its efficiency falls sooner.

The Fleet Law leads to the scaling efficiency metric for fixed global work. Substituting its step time (principle 10) for \(\MultiDeviceRunTime\) in equation 1, with \(\IronLawComputeTime\) standing for \(\SingleDeviceRunTime\) per step as noted there, gives equation 2:

\[ \ScalingEfficiency = \frac{\IronLawComputeTime}{\DeviceCount\times \DistributedStepTime(\DeviceCount)} = \frac{1}{1 + \frac{\DeviceCount(\CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount) + \SynchronizationTime(\DeviceCount) - \OverlappedTime)}{\IronLawComputeTime}} \tag{2}\]

This form states the scaling efficiency bound (principle 12), under which perfect linear scaling \((\ScalingEfficiency = 1.0)\) is a theoretical limit, not a practical target. Real systems fall short of it by an amount that depends on the configuration and grows with \(\DeviceCount\). The 175B data-parallel example below reaches only 15 percent, and the hybrid layouts later in the chapter recover much of that loss without reaching 1.0. The gap between \(\ScalingEfficiency = 1.0\) and the achieved efficiency is the price of distributed execution, paid in communication and coordination.

The equation also exposes the scaling wall. As \(\DeviceCount\) increases, the compute term \((\IronLawComputeTime/\DeviceCount)\) shrinks, but the communication and synchronization terms can remain constant or grow. A ring AllReduce’s bandwidth term, for example, stays near its bound however many workers join (principle 11). Eventually, the denominator is dominated by overhead, driving efficiency toward zero.

For data-parallel training of the 175B reference model, the per-step communication cost is dominated by the AllReduce of 350 GB of gradients, and how much of that exchange hides behind computation decides the efficiency. The scaling efficiency depends on computation per communicated byte. At fixed tokens per update, increasing parameter count raises both transformer computation and gradient bytes roughly proportionally, so model size alone does not guarantee better scaling. Larger batches raise computation per optimizer step without changing gradient size. The catch is that large batches can harm convergence and so require learning-rate tuning and warmup schedules. Network bandwidth enters directly because doubling InfiniBand bandwidth halves the bandwidth term, but efficiency changes according to the full step-time denominator rather than in direct proportion. The network fabric is therefore a first-order determinant of cluster productivity, not a secondary concern. Every point of scaling efficiency it recovers is returned across the much larger accelerator investment, which is why fabric spending is judged by the efficiency it buys rather than by its own price.

These factors interact, so efficiency cannot be inferred from model size or GPU count alone. The following worked model fixes the parameter count, tokens per update, accelerator count, effective bandwidth, local compute efficiency before communication, and overlap before calculating one efficiency point. Changing any of those inputs requires recomputing the full step-time model.

Napkin Math 6.5: Scaling efficiency for a 175B model
Setup: A communication-only model for training a 175B model on a DGX H100 cluster with 400 Gb/s InfiniBand per GPU. Pure replicated data parallelism is a counterfactual baseline here: the calculation isolates gradient exchange but does not establish that the full training state fits on each device.

  • Compute per step (assuming batch size 2M tokens, 6 FLOPs per parameter per token): \(\TrainingStepOperations = 6 \times 175 \times 10^9 \times 2 \times 10^6 \approx 2.1 \times 10^{18}\) FLOPs

  • Per-GPU compute time on 1,024 GPUs, each at 1,979 TFLOP/s peak in the 8-bit floating-point (FP8) format developed in section 6.4.8 (50 percent local compute efficiency before communication). The general matrix multiplications (GEMMs) run in FP8 while the gradients below still cross the wire in FP16, because the collective’s data type is chosen independently of the arithmetic’s: \(\IronLawComputeTime/\DeviceCount \approx 2.1\) seconds

  • AllReduce time (for 350 GB of gradients using ring-AllReduce with overlap): \(\ExposedCommunicationTime(\DeviceCount) \approx 11.9\) seconds (raw ring transfer takes \(2 \times (N-1)/N \times 350 / 50\), about 14 seconds; the schedule could hide 75 percent of that transfer, but the available compute window caps hidden communication at 15 percent of raw transfer time)

  • Scaling efficiency: \(\ScalingEfficiency = \PerDeviceComputeTime/(\PerDeviceComputeTime+ \ExposedCommunicationTime)\), which is equation 2 with the per-device compute window \(\PerDeviceComputeTime= \IronLawComputeTime/\DeviceCount\) and the exposed term standing for \(\CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount) + \SynchronizationTime(\DeviceCount) - \OverlappedTime\). Here the numerator is 2.1 s and the complete denominator is 14 s, giving 15 percent. The exposed communication term already subtracts the hidden portion from the raw transfer time and includes collective synchronization; subtracting overlap again would double-count the same saving.

Systems insight: The low efficiency (15 percent) shows why naive data parallelism at this scale is insufficient. It is one operating point, fixed by 2M per update, 1,024 GPUs, and 15 percent overlap, not a universal threshold.
