Another cluster size requires recomputing per-device compute time, collective cost, overlap, and topology. Hierarchy-aware systems recover much of the lost efficiency by combining data parallelism with tensor parallelism, which communicates over NVLink, and pipeline parallelism, which overlaps computation with communication.

↩︎ The compute window that bounds how much of a collective can hide is established in Overlap limits and exposed communication.

Parallelism-infrastructure interaction

The scaling efficiency of a run is set by the interaction between the model’s communication requirements and the infrastructure’s bandwidth hierarchy, not by the model architecture alone. Recovering the efficiency that pure data parallelism lost above means mapping each parallelism dimension onto the bandwidth tier that can carry its traffic. Section 6.6 works through three concrete configurations once tensor and pipeline parallelism have been developed.

The collective algorithm adds its own dependence on message size. Tree-based AllReduce reaches every rank in \(\mathcal{O}(\log \DeviceCount)\) steps and suits small, latency-bound messages, whereas large gradient buffers favor the bandwidth-optimal ring; message size, topology, and contention decide between them.

↩︎ The message size at which ring AllReduce overtakes tree AllReduce, derived from the latency and bandwidth terms of each algorithm, is established in The algorithm crossover point.

Even for pre-frontier transformers, those algorithm choices translate into substantial link-bandwidth requirements. BERT-Base (110M parameters) requires approximately 440 MB of gradient synchronization per iteration in FP32, while BERT-Large (340M parameters) requires approximately 1.4 GB. Because a ring AllReduce across 64 GPUs moves nearly twice the gradient bytes through each GPU’s link, finishing BERT-Large synchronization within 50 ms demands roughly 54 GB/s of sustained per-GPU bandwidth, and BERT-Base about a third of that. At 175B parameters, meeting the step budget requires combining 3D parallelism, gradient accumulation, and overlap rather than relying on a single flat collective, and whatever communication remains unhidden acts as the unscalable residual that Amdahl’s Law prices next.

6.4.2 Scaling physics and Amdahl’s Law with communication

Even a small unscalable cost in a single-device step eventually dominates distributed execution, because adding \(\DeviceCount\) devices shrinks the parallelizable compute share (\(\SingleDeviceRunTime / \DeviceCount\)) while leaving the fixed residual untouched. An Amdahl-style model separates that scalable work from the fixed residual fraction \(\SerialFraction\) of the single-device baseline (Amdahl's Law at fleet scale).

That baseline fraction is not the synchronization share a profiler reports for a distributed step (the distributed Amdahl pitfall of The Fleet Law), because the compute share has been divided by \(\DeviceCount\) while the residual has not. A residual of 1 percent of the single-device step occupies about 84 percent of each step on 512 GPUs. Reading the observed share as \(\SerialFraction\) would therefore overstate the residual many times over and predict a far lower speedup ceiling than the model implies.

Plotted on log-log axes up to 512 GPUs, the same fixed-residual model shows how much speedup each residual fraction gives up (figure 9), complementing the scaling efficiency bound (principle 12). In this illustrative model, a small baseline fraction (\(\SerialFraction= 0.1\%\)) stays closer to ideal scaling than a larger fraction (\(\SerialFraction= 1\%\)). A residual equal to a tenth of baseline time (\(\SerialFraction= 0.10\)) limits asymptotic speedup to \(1/\SerialFraction= 10\times\). Actual collective and coordination costs can change with worker count, so predicting a training run requires the full step-time accounting of equation 2.

Published benchmark runs show the same shortfall in practice. MLPerf Training times complete runs to a fixed quality target, and several submitters report the same system at several sizes within one round (Mattson et al. 2020; MLCommons 2025). Each of 5 such series for the GPT-3 and Llama 3.1 405B benchmarks falls below the ideal line drawn through its smallest configuration (figure 10). On NVIDIA’s Eos cluster, GPT-3 trained in about 58 minutes on 512 H100 GPUs and 3.9 minutes on 10,752. A 21× increase in accelerators therefore cut the time 14.9×, a scaling efficiency (equation 1, measured relative to the smallest configuration rather than one device) of 71 percent. Across the series, the largest configuration keeps 71–84 percent of ideal. Two effects compound in these numbers. Communication and coordination grow with worker count, and the larger configurations also train with larger global batches, which can require more samples to reach the target (section 6.4.6). Separating the two requires the step-level accounting of equation 2.

Mattson, Peter, Christine Cheng, Cody Coleman, Greg Diamos, Paulius Micikevicius, David Patterson, Hanlin Tang, et al. 2020. “MLPerf Training Benchmark.” Proceedings of Machine Learning and Systems (MLSys) 2: 336–49.

Predicting a run means pricing each term of equation 2 for the configuration at hand. For a large-message ring AllReduce, the communication term’s bandwidth contribution is \(\frac{2(\DeviceCount-1)}{\DeviceCount} \frac{\DataVolume}{\LinkBandwidth}\). This is the fleet counterpart of the single-machine iron law’s data-movement ratio \(\DataVolume/\Bandwidth\). Here \(\DataVolume\) is the payload being reduced (the gradient bytes \(\GradientPayload\) under data parallelism, or a shard of model state under sharding), and \(\LinkBandwidth\) is sustained per-link bandwidth. The expression omits per-message startup latency. Each wait must enter the accounting once.

↪ The single-machine iron law and its data-movement term are developed in Iron Law Mapping.

Communication energy climbs from HBM out to the network.

This communication overhead costs energy as well as wall-clock time, and the energy cost of communication grows with the physical distance between devices. At the scale of 10,000 GPUs, moving data is itself a power load. A full H100 NVLink envelope of roughly 9 PB/s at 7.5 pJ/bit represents about 540 kW before cooling and switch overhead. The smaller NDR InfiniBand envelope (500 TB/s at 35 pJ/bit) still represents about 140 kW. The per-boundary accounting behind these per-bit costs is developed in The Physics of Data Movement. Overlap hides communication time but not its energy, so avoiding unnecessary movement is the direct way to reduce the power term.

As the device count grows for a fixed workload, the balance can move from a linear scaling regime toward a communication-bound regime. The transition has no universal GPU count, because it depends on compute per device, message sizes, topology, and overlap. Coordination overhead becomes consequential when its exposed time is no longer small relative to useful computation. Measurements at the intended workload and placement must establish the efficiency curve.

More GPUs shrink each step’s compute, not its communication.

The efficiency metrics directly influence the choice of parallelism strategy. Data parallelism works well in the linear scaling regime but becomes communication bound at scale. The model-parallel strategies developed after this section replace part of that gradient traffic with other costs, which the same efficiency equation prices once each mechanism is in place. Hardware efficiency metrics govern throughput, yet convergence theory determines whether distributed training reaches the same solution quality as single-device training. Parallelism affects optimization convergence in three ways: convergence rate changes with batch size, adding workers yields diminishing returns beyond the critical batch size, and learning-rate schedules must be revalidated when the global batch changes.

6.4.3 Convergence rate for synchronous data parallel SGD

Averaging gradients across \(\DeviceCount\) workers processes \(\DeviceCount\) times as many examples per step, but only reduces the number of steps to convergence when gradient noise—rather than loss curvature—is what holds back a larger step size. Formally, let \(\LossFunction\) be lower bounded by \(\LossLowerBound\) and have \(\SmoothnessConstant\)-Lipschitz gradients, so the smoothness constant limits how sharply the gradient can change as parameters move. Each worker computes gradients from \(\LocalBatchSize\) independent samples. At update \(\OptimizerStepIndex\), the aggregated estimator \(\MinibatchGradient_t\), the average of the \(\DeviceCount\) workers’ local gradients, is unbiased, and its conditional variance satisfies \(\mathbb{E}[\|\MinibatchGradient_t-\nabla\LossFunction(\ModelParameters_t)\|^2\mid\ModelParameters_t]\leq\SampleGradientStandardDeviation^2/(\DeviceCount \LocalBatchSize)\). When averaging reduces that noise, synchronous SGD makes steady progress toward parameters where the gradient is small. These assumptions bound progress toward a small gradient norm, not convergence to a global minimizer or a particular validation accuracy.

Theorem 6.1: Convergence rate for distributed SGD
For the update \(\ModelParameters_{t+1}=\ModelParameters_t-\LearningRate\MinibatchGradient_t\) with constant step size \(0<\LearningRate\leq 1/\SmoothnessConstant\), after \(\OptimizerUpdateCount\) synchronized updates,

\[ \frac{1}{\OptimizerUpdateCount}\sum_{t=0}^{\OptimizerUpdateCount-1}\mathbb{E}\!\left[\|\nabla\LossFunction(\ModelParameters_t)\|^2\right] \leq \underbrace{\frac{2\bigl(\LossFunction(\ModelParameters_0)-\LossLowerBound\bigr)}{\LearningRate \OptimizerUpdateCount}}_{\text{optimization term}} + \underbrace{\frac{\LearningRate \SmoothnessConstant\SampleGradientStandardDeviation^2}{\DeviceCount \LocalBatchSize}}_{\text{stochastic-variance term}}. \]

Choosing \(\LearningRate=\min\!\left\{1/\SmoothnessConstant,\sqrt{2(\LossFunction(\ModelParameters_0)-\LossLowerBound)\DeviceCount \LocalBatchSize/(\SmoothnessConstant\SampleGradientStandardDeviation^2\OptimizerUpdateCount)}\right\}\) balances the two terms. Before the smoothness cap becomes active, the stochastic contribution scales as \(\mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{\DeviceCount \LocalBatchSize \OptimizerUpdateCount})\).

The theorem isolates the benefit that follows from the assumptions. With equal local batch size \(\LocalBatchSize\), synchronous data-parallel SGD computes the same minibatch estimator as one worker using batch size \(\DeviceCount\times \LocalBatchSize\) for that update, and the estimator variance decreases as \(1/(\DeviceCount\times \LocalBatchSize)\). It does not say that every increase in batch size proportionally reduces time to a target loss; learning-rate limits and critical-batch-size effects still apply. This distinction is the statistical efficiency of distributed training, separate from hardware efficiency. Because the theorem assumes perfect synchronization (BSP), convergence guarantees degrade as staleness increases whenever workers proceed at different rates or apply stale gradients.

6.4.4 Staleness impact across synchronization models

When a slow worker applies a gradient computed on an older parameter version, the update pushes the model along a direction that the current parameters may have already outgrown. The staleness parameter \(\GradientStaleness\) counts how many updates occur between computing and applying a gradient, tying the barrier savings of BSP, SSP, and ASP (section 6.3.1.5) to solution quality (Ho et al. 2013; Dutta et al. 2018). Because the damage from stale gradients depends on the objective, step-size schedule, worker count, and delay distribution, no single additive penalty applies across all runs.

Definition 6.5: Gradient staleness

Gradient staleness (\(\GradientStaleness\)) is the number of parameter updates that occur between the time a gradient is computed and the time it is applied to the global model state.

  1. Significance: It represents the synchronization error in distributed optimization. Increasing \(\GradientStaleness\) can improve throughput by reducing barrier waits \((\SynchronizationTime(\DeviceCount))\), but it typically degrades the rate of convergence, requiring more optimizer steps, and therefore more FLOPs, to reach the same accuracy.
  2. Distinction: Unlike network latency, which is a physical delay, staleness is an algorithmic offset that arises from the choice of synchronization protocol (e.g., ASP, SSP).
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that staleness is “always bad.” In reality, it is a throughput-convergence trade-off: for some large-scale workloads, allowing bounded staleness is one practical way to keep thousands of GPUs in use.

The convergence behavior differs across these models in ways that directly affect training cost and solution quality. In BSP (\(\GradientStaleness=0\)), all workers compute on the same parameter version and aggregate before updating. Under the assumptions of theorem 6.1, the synchronized estimator inherits the \(\DeviceCount\times \LocalBatchSize\) minibatch guarantee.

SSP (\(\GradientStaleness\leq \StalenessBound\)) allows workers to proceed up to \(\StalenessBound\) iterations ahead of the slowest worker. Ho et al. (2013) bound the average regret of their SSP update sequence at \(\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{(\StalenessBound+1)\DeviceCount/\OptimizerUpdateCount})\) for \(\DeviceCount\) workers and \(\OptimizerUpdateCount\) updates. Average regret is the per-update gap between the loss its iterates incur and the loss of the best fixed parameters chosen in hindsight. The bound assumes convex Lipschitz component functions, a bounded domain, and a decreasing step size. It makes the staleness dependence explicit, since the bound grows with \(\StalenessBound\). It measures a different quantity from the gradient-norm bound of theorem 6.1, which covers nonconvex objectives, so the two rates cannot be compared directly.

Dutta, Sanghamitra, Gauri Joshi, Soumyadip Ghosh, Parijat Dube, and Priya Nagpurkar. 2018. “Slow and Stale Gradients Can Win the Race: Error-Runtime Trade-Offs in Distributed SGD.” Proceedings of the Twenty-First International Conference on Artificial Intelligence and Statistics 84: 803–12.

Asynchronous SGD imposes no protocol-wide finite delay bound. Removing the barrier can improve update throughput, but a convergence rate requires additional assumptions about realized delays, the objective, and the step-size schedule. Dutta et al. (2018) analyze this as an error-runtime trade-off in which worker speed and gradient staleness jointly determine whether accepting a delayed update helps. Therefore neither a fixed square-of-average-delay penalty nor the complete loss of \(\DeviceCount\)-worker variance reduction follows from asynchronous execution alone. The protocol fixes each model’s delay control, but the strength of its convergence guarantee depends on further assumptions (table 4).

6.4.5 Learning rate scaling rules

Even under zero-staleness BSP (\(\GradientStaleness=0\) in table 4), scaling out across \(\DeviceCount\) workers changes the optimization trajectory whenever the global batch grows with the cluster. Multiplying the global batch by \(\BatchScalingFactor\) cuts the number of optimizer updates per epoch by the same factor, so keeping the single-GPU learning rate leaves the model under-trained, while jumping straight to a \(\BatchScalingFactor\)-fold larger step size can destabilize early epochs. Whenever data parallelism changes the effective batch size, the learning-rate schedule must be revalidated against the target quality; two scaling rules provide starting points for that adjustment.

The linear scaling rule (Goyal et al. 2017) proposes that when batch size is multiplied by \(\BatchScalingFactor\), learning rate also increases by \(\BatchScalingFactor\):

\[ \ScaledLearningRate = \BatchScalingFactor\cdot \BaseLearningRate\]

The approximation compares one large-batch step at learning rate \(\BatchScalingFactor\LearningRate\) with \(\BatchScalingFactor\) consecutive small-batch steps at learning rate \(\LearningRate\). At batch size \(\BatchScalingFactor\times \BatchSize\), independent gradient contributions reduce estimator variance by factor \(\BatchScalingFactor\), but lower variance alone does not establish stability. The approximation also requires gradients to change little over the corresponding \(\BatchScalingFactor\) small-batch steps. Goyal et al. (2017) validated the recipe for ResNet-50/ImageNet at global batch size 8,192 with gradual warmup. Layer-wise adaptive rate scaling (LARS) and layer-wise adaptive moments based optimizer (LAMB) provide related large-batch approaches for ImageNet and BERT-style pretraining (You et al. 2017; You et al. 2020). A warmup period increases the learning rate from \(\BaseLearningRate\) to \(\BatchScalingFactor\cdot \BaseLearningRate\) over the first \(\WarmupStepCount\) iterations to reduce early instability; the schedule still requires validation:

\[ \LearningRate_t = \BaseLearningRate+ \frac{\OptimizerStepIndex}{\WarmupStepCount}(\BatchScalingFactor\cdot \BaseLearningRate- \BaseLearningRate) \quad \text{for } \OptimizerStepIndex< \WarmupStepCount\]

The rule and the warmup were introduced together, and the run that introduced them shows why the scaled learning rate needs the warmup. A learning-rate schedule tuned for an eight-GPU run need not reach the same accuracy at 256 GPUs once the global batch grows with the worker count.

War Story 6.1: The one-hour ImageNet run (2017)
Context: Facebook AI Research set out to train ResNet-50 on ImageNet in one hour using 256 GPUs, pushing data parallelism far beyond the batch sizes that earlier practice treated as safe (Goyal et al. 2017).

Mechanism: Scaling the global batch to 8,192 cut the number of optimizer updates per epoch by the same factor. Keeping the small-batch learning rate left too few effective updates to reach the target accuracy, while scaling the learning rate up by the same factor destabilized optimization in the first epochs, when the loss surface changes fastest.

Impact: Without the gradual warmup recipe, the large-batch runs reported higher training and validation error than the small-batch baseline. Higher hardware throughput alone did not preserve the quality target.

Fix: The team introduced the linear learning-rate scaling rule paired with a 5-epoch gradual warmup schedule, stabilizing optimization at batch size 8,192.

Systems lesson: Distributed training demands more than parallel hardware alone. Scaling changes the optimization regime, so the cluster, communication schedule, batch size, and learning-rate schedule must be tuned as one system.

The square root scaling rule is an alternative learning-rate heuristic to test when increasing batch size:

\[ \ScaledLearningRate = \sqrt{\BatchScalingFactor} \cdot \BaseLearningRate\]

This rule increases the learning rate more slowly than linear scaling. Its suitability depends on the optimizer, schedule, and training regime; exceeding a critical-batch estimate does not make it mandatory or guarantee convergence. The learning rate must be validated against the same target quality.

LARS (You et al. 2017) scales each layer’s update relative to its weight norm. The simplified LARS expression below illustrates the ratio of weight norm to gradient norm with weight decay. LAMB (You et al. 2020) applies a related layer-wise adaptation to an Adam-based update rather than using this raw-gradient denominator:

\[ \LearningRate_\ell = \LarsGlobalLearningRate\cdot \frac{\|\LayerWeights_\ell\|}{\|\MinibatchGradient_\ell\| + \WeightPenaltyStrength\|\LayerWeights_\ell\|} \]

Here \(\WeightPenaltyStrength\) is the weight-decay coefficient in the illustrated LARS expression, distinct from the failure-rate parameter used in reliability analysis. Layer-wise scaling controls update magnitude relative to parameter magnitude, but does not guarantee convergence for arbitrary batches. Its systems role is to extend the range of validated batch sizes over which added data-parallel workers can reduce time to quality.

6.4.6 Critical batch size and diminishing returns

Increasing the global batch eventually yields diminishing reductions in the optimization steps needed to reach a fixed target quality. The critical batch size \(\CriticalBatchSize\) marks the transition point beyond which increasing batch size yields diminishing returns in convergence per sample seen.

Definition 6.6: Critical batch size

Critical batch size \((\CriticalBatchSize)\) is a workload-dependent crossover batch size beyond which further batch growth yields diminishing reductions in optimization steps per added sample (McCandlish et al. 2018; Shallue et al. 2019).

  1. Significance: It characterizes statistical scaling, separately from hardware throughput. In the gradient-noise model, batches well below \(\CriticalBatchSize\) can reduce step count approximately in proportion to batch growth; above it, additional samples buy progressively smaller step-count reductions.
  2. Distinction: Unlike the memory-limited batch size (determined by memory capacity and activation footprint), the critical batch size depends on the model, data, optimizer, training phase, and target quality.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that training can be sped up indefinitely by adding GPUs. In reality, \(\CriticalBatchSize\) is a guide to diminishing statistical returns, not a hard physical ceiling or a universal optimum in wall-clock time.
Shallue, Christopher J, Jaehoon Lee, Joseph Antognini, Jascha Sohl-Dickstein, Roy Frostig, and George E Dahl. 2019. “Measuring the Effects of Data Parallelism on Neural Network Training.” Journal of Machine Learning Research 20: 1–49.

The gradient-noise-scale proxy for the critical batch size can be estimated as:

\[ \CriticalBatchSize\approx \frac{\text{tr}(\GradientCovarianceMatrix)}{\|\nabla \LossFunction(\ModelParameters)\|^2} \]

where \(\text{tr}(\GradientCovarianceMatrix)\) is the trace of the gradient covariance matrix (total gradient variance) and \(\|\nabla \LossFunction(\ModelParameters)\|^2\) is the squared gradient norm (signal strength). Intuitively, \(\CriticalBatchSize\) is the batch size at which averaging reduces gradient variance to the level of the true gradient magnitude.

Published large-batch regimes illustrate the scale rather than providing fixed thresholds. On ImageNet, Goyal et al. (2017) stabilized ResNet-50 at global batch 8,192 with warmup, and LARS extends ImageNet large-batch training further in some settings (You et al. 2017). For BERT-Large pretraining, LAMB reports training at 32K–64K batch sizes with layer-wise adaptive updates (You et al. 2020). Across other domains, McCandlish et al. (2018) show useful batch-size limits ranging from tens of thousands in ImageNet-like settings to millions in reinforcement-learning workloads, so new frontier-language-model thresholds should be measured rather than copied.

Goyal, Priya, Piotr Dollár, Ross Girshick, Pieter Noordhuis, Lukasz Wesolowski, Aapo Kyrola, Andrew Tulloch, Yangqing Jia, and Kaiming He. 2017. “Accurate, Large Minibatch SGD: Training ImageNet in 1 Hour.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1706.02677 abs/1706.02677.
You, Yang, Igor Gitman, and Boris Ginsburg. 2017. “Large Batch Training of Convolutional Networks.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1708.03888.
You, Y., J. Li, S. Reddi, J. Hseu, S. Kumar, S. Bhojanapalli, X. Song, J. Demmel, K. Keutzer, and C.-J. Hsieh. 2020. “Large Batch Optimization for Deep Learning: Training BERT in 76 Minutes.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

The gradient-noise model separates statistical efficiency from hardware efficiency. Well below \(\CriticalBatchSize\), increasing batch size can reduce optimization steps almost proportionally while preserving similar total sample use. Near \(\CriticalBatchSize\), the trade-off between fewer updates and more samples becomes material. Above it, step count approaches a lower limit while sample use continues to increase. Neither maximum samples per second nor minimum training time necessarily occurs at \(\CriticalBatchSize\): those objectives also depend on device utilization, communication, and the validated learning-rate schedule.

On log-log axes, the gradient-noise trade-off appears as a divergence between optimization step count and total sample consumption (figure 11). Below the critical threshold (\(\BatchSize/\CriticalBatchSize< 1\)), increasing batch size yields near-linear reductions in optimization steps with negligible sample waste. Past the crossover point at \(\BatchSize/\CriticalBatchSize= 1\) (where both normalized costs equal 2), the step count curve asymptotically flattens toward 1 while total examples processed climb steeply along the \(1 + \BatchSize/\CriticalBatchSize\) trajectory.

Dividing the critical batch by the per-worker batch \(\LocalBatchSize\) estimates where worker growth leaves the near-proportional statistical scaling regime. With one local batch per update and its size held fixed, the crossover occurs around \(\DeviceCount\approx \CriticalBatchSize/\LocalBatchSize\) workers. Past that point, additional workers mainly raise sample throughput that the optimizer cannot convert into proportionally fewer steps. Gradient accumulation increases each worker’s contribution per update and lowers the corresponding worker count. Shrinking \(\LocalBatchSize\) moves the crossover outward but leaves less computation per worker to hide communication, coupling statistical and hardware efficiency.

That crossover informs a cost-time trade-off rather than setting a mandatory worker count. Larger batches may still reduce time to target even as they consume more examples. That benefit must be established with measured step time and a validated learning-rate schedule at the same target quality. Changing synchronization or compression also changes the optimization process, so its communication savings must be weighed against any additional steps required.

Checkpoint 6.2: Scaling decisions

Given a 7B-parameter model distributed across a cluster of 64 A100 GPUs (80 GB HBM2e each), estimate the critical-batch crossover. Use a pilot trace with \(\text{tr}(\GradientCovarianceMatrix)=1.6 \times 10^6\), \(\|\nabla \LossFunction(\ModelParameters)\|^2=100\), per-GPU batch \(\LocalBatchSize=4\), and a proposed gradient-accumulation depth of 32 steps, giving \(\GlobalBatchSize= 64 \times 4 \times 32 = 8{,}192\) samples.

6.4.7 Communication cost versus convergence speed

Communication savings matter only through the resulting time and cost to reach target model quality. Scaling a transformer language model from 8 to 64 workers, with a per-worker batch of \(\LocalBatchSize= 32\), shows how shorter steps and extra iterations to the same target combine into that cost.

Napkin Math 6.6: Scaling from 8 to 64 workers
Setup: This illustrative comparison assumes the iteration counts needed to reach one fixed target quality. They are scenario inputs, not measured convergence results or predictions from GPU count. The baseline is 100K iterations on single GPU with \(\LocalBatchSize=\) 32.

8 workers (BSP)

  • Effective batch size: \(\BatchSize=\) 8 \(\times\) 32 = 256
  • Learning rate: \(\LearningRate=\) 8 \(\times \BaseLearningRate\) (illustrative candidate with warmup; validate for the workload)
  • Expected iterations: 100K \(/\) 8 = 12.5K iterations
  • Assumed iterations to the same target: 12.8K iterations (98 percent efficiency)
  • Communication overhead: 15 percent (NVLink intra-node)
  • Wall-clock speedup: 100K \(\times\) 1 \(/\) (12.8K \(\times\) 1.15×) = 6.8×

64 workers (BSP)

  • Effective batch size: \(\BatchSize=\) 64 \(\times\) 32 = 2,048
  • Learning rate (\(\DeviceCount=\) 64 workers): \(\LearningRate= \DeviceCount\times \BaseLearningRate\) is the scenario’s assumed candidate schedule, not a prescription determined by \(\CriticalBatchSize\) alone
  • Assuming \(\CriticalBatchSize\approx\) 4,000 (below critical): the useful-worker bound is \(\CriticalBatchSize/\LocalBatchSize\approx\) 4,000 \(/\) 32 \(\approx\) 125 workers, so 64 workers stay inside the near-proportional regime; this estimate does not validate a learning-rate schedule
  • Expected iterations: 100K \(/\) 64 = 1.56K iterations
  • Assumed iterations to the same target: 1.72K iterations (91 percent efficiency)
  • Communication overhead: 45 percent (InfiniBand inter-node, 8 nodes)
  • Wall-clock speedup: 100K \(\times\) 1 \(/\) (1.72K \(\times\) 1.45×) = 40.1×

SSP workers: 64 workers (\(\StalenessBound=\) 4)

  • Same effective batch size: \(\BatchSize=\) 2,048
  • Illustrative learning-rate reduction, not a staleness theorem: \(\StalenessAdjustedLearningRate= \BspLearningRate/ \sqrt{1 + \StalenessBound}\) with \(\StalenessBound=\) 4, giving \(\StalenessAdjustedLearningRate\approx\) 0.45 \(\times \BspLearningRate\)
  • Expected iterations: Higher due to staleness penalty
  • Assumed iterations to the same target: 2.1K iterations (74 percent efficiency)
  • Communication overhead: 25 percent (reduced synchronization)
  • Wall-clock speedup: 100K \(\times\) 1 \(/\) (2.1K \(\times\) 1.25×) = 38.1×

Analysis (table 5)

The 64-GPU BSP configuration achieves 40× speedup despite only 91 percent sample efficiency because the communication overhead (45 percent) is offset by the larger worker count. SSP at 64 workers comes close in wall-clock time with lower per-iteration communication (25 percent), but the extra iterations its stale updates require hold it to 38.1× against BSP’s 40.1×. In this scenario it therefore finishes slightly later and costs slightly more than 64-worker BSP.

Cost Analysis (assuming $2/GPU-hour):

  • 8: 12.8K iters \(\times\) 0.46 s/iter \(\times\) 8/3600 \(\times\) $2/GPU-hour = $26
  • 64 BSP: 1.72K iters \(\times\) 0.58 s/iter \(\times\) 64/3600 \(\times\) $2/GPU-hour = $35
  • 64 SSP: 2.1K iters \(\times\) 0.50 s/iter \(\times\) 64/3600 \(\times\) $2/GPU-hour = $37

Despite higher parallelism, 64-GPU training costs more per run due to communication overhead and reduced sample efficiency. The 8-GPU configuration is more cost-efficient, but its 6.8× speedup against the 64-GPU run’s 40.1× leaves it several times slower in wall-clock time. The choice depends on whether minimizing cost or minimizing time-to-result is the priority.

On the cost-time plane of figure 12, 64-worker BSP dominates 64-worker SSP on both axes. SSP saves communication per iteration, but its stale updates need enough extra iterations to cost more and finish later, while 8-worker BSP remains the cheaper and slower option.

Moving a job along this cost-time frontier requires shrinking each message, synchronizing less often, or relaxing the global reduction topology. Gradient compression shrinks the payload directly by transmitting quantized, sparsified, or low-rank approximations. Examples include sign-based updates (Bernstein et al. 2018), Deep Gradient Compression (Lin et al. 2018), PowerSGD (Vogels et al. 2019), quantized SGD with convergence analysis (Alistarh et al. 2017), and empirical sparse gradient dropping for neural machine translation (Aji and Heafield 2017).

Bernstein, Jeremy, Yu-Xiang Wang, Kamyar Azizzadenesheli, and Animashree Anandkumar. 2018. “signSGD: Compressed Optimisation for Non-Convex Problems.” International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML) 80: 560–69.
Lin, Yujun, Song Han, Huizi Mao, Yu Wang, and William J Dally. 2018. “Deep Gradient Compression: Reducing the Communication Bandwidth for Distributed Training.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Vogels, Thijs, Sai Praneeth Karimireddy, and Martin Jaggi. 2019. “PowerSGD: Practical Low-Rank Gradient Compression for Distributed Optimization.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 32.
Alistarh, Dan, Demjan Grubic, Jerry Z. Li, Ryota Tomioka, and Milan Vojnovic. 2017. “QSGD: Communication-Efficient SGD via Gradient Quantization and Encoding.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 30: 1709–20.
Aji, Alham Fikri, and Kenneth Heafield. 2017. “Sparse Communication for Distributed Gradient Descent.” Proceedings of the 2017 Conference on Empirical Methods in Natural Language Processing, 440–45. https://doi.org/10.18653/v1/d17-1045.

↩︎ Quantized, sparsified, and low-rank gradient compression, the error feedback that keeps them convergent, and the criteria for when they pay, are established in Gradient Compression Under Bandwidth Scarcity.

Stich, Sebastian U. 2019. “Local SGD Converges Fast and Communicates Little.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

Instead of shrinking each payload, Local SGD lowers synchronization frequency by letting workers perform \(\LocalUpdateCount\) local updates before averaging, which cuts collective calls per local update by a factor of \(\LocalUpdateCount\). Results for smooth, strongly convex objectives can recover synchronous SGD’s asymptotic rate under specified learning-rate and local-update-interval conditions (Stich 2019), so the synchronization interval remains part of the convergence contract rather than an unrestricted communication saving.

Where Local SGD keeps a global AllReduce at wider intervals, Decentralized SGD replaces the global collective with sparse neighbor exchanges along a communication graph. Immediate bandwidth demand drops, at the cost of taking additional rounds to spread information across the group. Graph connectivity, link service time, and the optimization effect of delayed mixing determine whether that trade-off helps a geographically distributed workload.

Choosing among these three levers depends on which bottleneck binds and how many extra iterations the altered update costs. Each candidate must be judged by measured time to the same target quality. A narrower numeric format offers one more way to shrink the payload without changing the collective schedule, under conditions that FP8 makes precise.

6.4.8 FP8 for distributed training

Running matrix multiplications in 8-bit floating point (FP8) accelerates local compute, which can make a distributed job more communication-bound if gradients still cross the network in 16-bit precision. Compute precision and communication precision are separate choices: casting GEMM operands to half as many bytes reduces operand storage and local memory traffic, but it halves a collective payload only when the communication path explicitly transmits that narrower representation.

↩︎ The throughput that narrower precisions buy on current accelerators is established in Precision and throughput trade-offs.

FP8 matters to distributed training only after four dtypes are distinguished: the stored parameter dtype, the GEMM operand dtype, the accumulator or master-state dtype, and the collective wire dtype. Traditional mixed-precision training commonly retains FP32 optimizer or master state while using FP16/BF16 operands. FP8-capable accelerators add E4M3 (four exponent bits and three mantissa bits) and E5M2 (five exponent bits and two mantissa bits) operand formats optimized for different tensor distributions (Micikevicius et al. 2022).

The two FP8 formats trade precision against range. E4M3 keeps an extra mantissa bit for scaled weights and activations, which cluster within a predictable range, while E5M2 keeps an extra exponent bit for scaled gradients, which can span many orders of magnitude during backpropagation. Because tensor magnitudes drift during training, each tensor is multiplied before conversion by a scale, commonly derived from its recent maximum absolute value, a policy called dynamic scaling. Neither the format nor the scaling policy guarantees that every value survives conversion, so training quality must be validated for each workload.

↪ Per-parameter training memory under each of these formats is collected for reference in Numeric Precision and Training Memory.

Micikevicius, Paulius, Dusan Stosic, Neil Burgess, Marius Cornea, Pradeep Dubey, Richard Grisenthwaite, Sangwon Ha, et al. 2022. “FP8 Formats for Deep Learning.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2209.05433.

An FP8 training contract pairs FP32 master or optimizer state and FP8 GEMM operands with higher-precision accumulation, and it can approach FP16 training quality on suitable workloads (Micikevicius et al. 2022). FP8 halves bytes only for tensors actually stored or transmitted in FP8, so a collective that reduces FP16 gradients still moves FP16 bytes even when every preceding GEMM ran in FP8. FP8 therefore earns its place in distributed training when lower-precision compute shortens the local critical path, or when an explicit communication cast shrinks payloads while the accumulation and scaling policy protects convergence. It changes representation, not the parallelization axis. Compression, local updates, and narrower formats all make each data-parallel step cheaper, yet none of them partitions an individual layer or its activations; that remaining capacity constraint motivates model parallelism.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A team scales a data-parallel training run from 8 to 64 workers, increasing the effective global batch size from 512 to 4,096 samples. If the model operates comfortably below its critical batch size, which learning-rate adjustment follows the linear scaling heuristic?

    1. Keep the learning rate unchanged, because large-batch variance reduction automatically stabilizes optimization
    2. Multiply the learning rate by 64 to match the total worker count rather than the batch expansion ratio
    3. Multiply the learning rate by \(\sqrt{8}\) from step zero without a warmup schedule
    4. Multiply the learning rate by \(8\times\) (matching the batch expansion ratio) and include a gradual linear warmup schedule

  2. Explain the distinction between hardware efficiency and statistical efficiency in distributed training, and describe a concrete scenario where a cluster configuration achieves high hardware efficiency but poor statistical efficiency.

  3. Why must the learning-rate schedule be revalidated when moving from Bulk-Synchronous Parallel (BSP) to Stale-Synchronous Parallel (SSP) at the same global batch size?

    1. Gradients computed against stale parameters can alter update stability, with effects that depend on delay, objective, and optimizer schedule
    2. SSP workers compute exact full-batch gradients instead of stochastic minibatches
    3. SSP eliminates all inter-GPU network communication, which alters the loss landscape
    4. SSP is mathematically restricted to second-order quasi-Newton optimizers that require small step sizes

  4. The threshold batch size \(B^* \approx \frac{\text{tr}(\Sigma)}{\|\nabla \mathcal{L}(\theta)\|^2}\), representing the point where gradient variance equals true gradient magnitude and past which larger batches yield diminishing returns in sample efficiency, is known as the ____.

  5. Why does the FP8 recipe described in this chapter pair the E4M3 format for forward-pass activations and weights with the E5M2 format for backward-pass gradients?

    1. E4M3 has a wider numerical range than E5M2, which prevents gradient underflow during backpropagation
    2. E4M3 spends more bits on the mantissa, giving finer precision for bounded weights and activations, while E5M2 spends more bits on the exponent, giving the wider dynamic range that small and widely varying gradients need
    3. E4M3 is an integer format while E5M2 is a floating-point format supported exclusively on CPU hosts
    4. E5M2 requires half the memory bandwidth of E4M3, accelerating the backward pass AllReduce

  6. Explain why per-tensor dynamic scaling is necessary in FP8 distributed training and describe how the scaling factor is computed during execution.

See Answers →

6.5 Model Parallelism

Sharded data parallelism distributes stored state but still asks each worker to execute every layer on its local examples. If one layer’s gathered parameters and working memory exceed device capacity, or its execution is too slow for the target schedule, splitting that computation becomes a candidate. Model parallelism addresses this form of the memory capacity gap (principle 2) by assigning different computations to different devices. Its value must be compared with offload, recomputation, and smaller local batches under the same throughput and quality requirements.

Definition 6.7: Model parallelism

Model parallelism is a distributed training strategy that partitions a single neural network’s parameters or operations across multiple devices so each device computes a distinct portion of the model.

  1. Significance: If a model with parameter state \(\WeightMemory\) is split across \(\TensorParallelDegree \PipelineParallelDegree\) model-parallel devices, the ideal per-device parameter footprint falls toward \(\WeightMemory/(\TensorParallelDegree \PipelineParallelDegree)\), enabling training of models that exceed single-device memory. The cost is extra communication for activations and gradients plus idle time from sequential dependencies, so the memory gain must exceed the transfer and pipeline-bubble overhead introduced by the partition.
  2. Distinction: Unlike data parallelism, which replicates the full model on every worker and shards the minibatch, model parallelism shards the model itself. Pipeline parallelism and tensor parallelism are implementation forms of model parallelism: one partitions layers into stages, while the other partitions individual tensor operations.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that model parallelism automatically speeds up training. Its primary benefit is capacity, not throughput; without careful scheduling and overlap, downstream devices wait for upstream activations and utilization can fall below a single-device baseline.

Weights are only a small slice of the full Adam training state.

Memory fit alone does not settle whether a model needs this kind of partition. For a 175B-parameter model, weights alone occupy 350 GB, or about 5.5 GB per GPU across a 64-GPU group, one-sixteenth of the 1,024-GPU reference cluster. Full mixed-precision Adam training state is much larger, 2,800 GB globally, yet ZeRO-3 across the same 64 GPUs reduces it to roughly 43.8 GB per GPU before activations, which fits an 80 GB accelerator. ZeRO-3 can therefore make the static state fit, as the notebook below confirms. It pays for that fit in traffic, because every step regathers every layer’s parameters across the data-parallel fabric, roughly three times the model’s parameter bytes per worker (section 6.3.3). It also cannot split an individual layer’s computation or activations across devices. Tensor parallelism addresses both limits by partitioning individual weight matrices across devices, using the column-then-row pairing of figure 16, detailed in section 6.5.3, which confines collective communication to block boundaries. Pipeline parallelism instead stages sequential layers across devices (section 6.5.2).

Activations add a separate memory requirement. Under the earlier unchecked-storage assumptions, a 2048-token sequence through 175B parameters produces roughly 1.1 TB at a local batch of 4 sequences; optimizer sharding does not reduce that storage. Recomputation trades memory for extra FLOPs, partitioning distributes activations, and offload moves them to another memory tier. Kernel and batch-size choices also change the budget. Full activation checkpointing brings the same modeled case down to ~19.3 GB. The following notebook, which uses a single-sequence local batch, checks whether ZeRO-3 plus checkpointing fits the model without partitioning its computation.

Napkin Math 6.7: The memory wall of scale
Problem: Training a 175B-parameter model (like GPT-3) on a 64-GPU group of NVIDIA A100s (80 GB), smaller than the 1,024-GPU reference cluster. The A100 carries the same 80 GB of HBM as the reference H100, so the capacity verdict carries over, and a small group is the harder test of whether sharding alone suffices. Can data parallelism with ZeRO-3 handle this?

Math:

  1. Parameter storage: 175B params \(\times\) 2 bytes (FP16) = 350 GB.
  2. Gradients and optimizer state: Gradients add 175B params \(\times\) 2 bytes = 350 GB, while Adam FP32 master weights, momentum, and variance add 175B params \(\times\) 12 bytes = 2,100 GB.
  3. Total static memory: 2,800 GB.
  4. ZeRO-3 sharding: With 64 GPUs, per-GPU static memory = 2,800 GB/64 GPUs \(\approx\) 43.8 GB.
  5. Activation memory: For sequence length 2048 and batch size 1, the canonical full-checkpointing strategy stores \(\approx\) 4.8 GB of activations per GPU.

Systems insight: 43.8 GB (static) + 4.8 GB (dynamic) = 48.6 GB. Under this simplified accounting, ZeRO-3 plus full activation checkpointing fits within the 80 GB A100 capacity, leaving material headroom for framework buffers and transient layer gathers. If those additional allocations exceed the remaining HBM, the design can reduce the local batch, offload state, increase the sharding degree, or add tensor or pipeline parallelism; tensor parallelism is not the only recourse.

In this example, sharding and checkpointing make the modeled state fit, leaving two distinct reasons to partition the model itself. One is regather traffic: on the 1,024-GPU reference cluster, ZeRO-3 moves about 1.1 TB per worker per step for this model, which on one NDR InfiniBand port takes about 10× as long as the step’s computation. The other is layer execution, since full sharding gathers parameters on demand without ever splitting a single layer’s computation or activations across devices. Model parallelism instead cuts the computation graph directly, either between sequential layer groups (such as layers 1–4 and layers 5–8) or inside a layer across channels and attention heads. Layer cuts introduce pipeline dependencies that leave downstream devices idle while an upstream stage computes, creating the pipeline bubble that governs utilization for this partition. Channel and head cuts instead expose concurrent work within a layer at the price of intra-layer exchanges. Other workloads still need these cuts to fit peak memory capacity.

These cuts enable the large-scale cases that exceed a single device for different reasons. GPT-3, with 175B parameters, was trained with model parallelism within each matrix multiplication and across layers (Brown et al. 2020), avoiding a full-parameter regather on every step. Vision transformers processing high-resolution \(16\Glyph{k}{\times}16\Glyph{k}\) pixel images use model parallelism to bound activation memory. Mixture-of-experts (MoE) architectures, whose sparsely activated feed-forward blocks are developed in section 6.5.5, distribute their conditional computation paths across hardware (Shazeer et al. 2017; Lepikhin et al. 2021; Fedus et al. 2022).

Brown, Tom B., Benjamin Mann, Nick Ryder, Melanie Subbiah, Jared Kaplan, Prafulla Dhariwal, Arvind Neelakantan, et al. 2020. “Language Models Are Few-Shot Learners.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 33: 1877–901. https://doi.org/10.48550/arxiv.2005.14165.

Each partitioning strategy trades single-device memory overflow for a different systems cost: layer-wise partitioning pays in idle time, pipeline parallelism pays in scheduling complexity to hide that idle time, and tensor parallelism pays in NVLink bandwidth to split individual layers. Sequence and context parallelism, expert parallelism, and sparse embedding sharding then extend the same trade-off to long-context activations, sparsely activated experts, and sparsely touched embedding tables.

Checkpoint 6.3: Model parallelism foundations

Verify your understanding of model sharding:

6.5.1 Layer-wise partitioning

Where ZeRO-3 met the capacity limit by sharding storage while every worker still executed every layer, layer-wise model parallelism gives each device a unique contiguous block of layers to compute. It is the least invasive model cut: with 8-way partitioning, the 175B-parameter model’s 350 GB of FP16 weights fall to approximately 44 GB per GPU, though activations, gradients, and optimizer state still need sharding, offload, or pipeline scheduling (section 6.6).

Because consecutive layer blocks remain sequentially dependent, however, a single batch passing through those stages in order leaves every other GPU waiting for its turn. In a 16-layer transformer placed four consecutive layers per GPU (figure 13), panel (a) shows each device holding one-quarter of the weights and exchanging only boundary activations and gradients, while panel (b) reveals the utilization penalty: without microbatch pipelining, each GPU computes in only two of the eight schematic time slots and sits idle for the other six.

Partitioning layers changes where an optimizer update runs without necessarily changing the update rule. Each GPU can apply Adam (Kingma and Ba 2015), AdaFactor, or another optimizer to its parameter slice once the required gradients are complete. A device holding transformer layers 1–12 updates those layers; tied parameters, global gradient clipping, or a combined data-parallel group can still require coordination. Preserving synchronous optimization also requires the schedule to use consistent parameter versions. Sequential dependencies introduce pipeline bubbles, and unequal stage service times add waiting. The engineering problem is to overlap microbatches and balance stage work while preserving that update contract (Rasley et al. 2020).

Kingma, Diederik P., and Jimmy Ba. 2015. “Adam: A Method for Stochastic Optimization.” 3rd International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

6.5.2 Pipeline parallelism

Without pipelining, a four-stage layer partition leaves three of the four GPUs idle at any moment because downstream stages cannot start until upstream stages finish the entire batch (figure 13). Pipeline parallelism breaks that stall by splitting the batch into smaller microbatches and streaming them through the stages like a relay race. As soon as GPU 1 finishes the initial transformer blocks on microbatch 1, it hands the resulting activation tensor to GPU 2 and immediately starts microbatch 2. For the 175B model with hidden dimension 12,288 and a microbatch of 4 sequences at 2,048 tokens each, that forward handoff carries approximately 201 MB per stage boundary per microbatch. GPU 2 needs that payload before processing the same microbatch, creating a dependency chain through the stages, while other microbatches occupy the remaining stages concurrently. Backward propagates activation gradients in reverse as each stage computes gradients for its own parameters.

Definition 6.8: Pipeline parallelism

Pipeline parallelism is a model parallelism technique that partitions a neural network’s layers into sequential stages assigned to different devices, passing activations forward and gradients backward between stages while overlapping computation across stages using microbatches to maintain throughput.

  1. Significance: Each boundary transfers forward activations and backward activation gradients. A forward tensor contains \(\MicrobatchSize \times \SequenceLength\times \HiddenDimension\times 2\) bytes at BF16, where \(\MicrobatchSize\) is the microbatch size. For a hidden dimension of 8,192 with microbatch size 1 and a 2,048-token sequence, the forward payload is approximately 8,192 \(\times\) 2,048 \(\times\) 2 \(\approx\) 34 MB per microbatch per boundary. Backward traffic and all microbatches must be counted before comparing this with a per-update gradient AllReduce or sizing a 50 GB/s InfiniBand link. In a balanced noninterleaved schedule with ideal transfers, the idle fraction is approximately \((\PipelineParallelDegree-1)/(\MicrobatchCount+\PipelineParallelDegree-1)\), where \(\PipelineParallelDegree\) is the stage count and \(\MicrobatchCount\) the microbatch count.
  2. Distinction: Tensor parallelism partitions operations within a layer and introduces communication among their participants. Pipeline parallelism instead transfers activations and their gradients between sequential stages. This coarser exchange can suit slower links when the transfer fits within the stage schedule; the interconnect name alone does not establish feasibility.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that adding pipeline stages always improves throughput. Each additional stage increases the pipeline bubble fraction \((\PipelineParallelDegree-1)/(\MicrobatchCount+\PipelineParallelDegree-1)\) unless the number of microbatches \(\MicrobatchCount\) scales proportionally, causing hardware to idle and erasing the benefit of deeper partitioning.

In the GPipe schedule of figure 14, a four-stage pipeline processes four microbatches (\(\PipelineParallelDegree=4, \MicrobatchCount=4\)). Dividing the global batch into discrete microbatches allows forward passes to overlap diagonally across stages before backward passes unfold in reverse order. While microbatching raises hardware utilization above naive sequential placement, pipeline fill (\(\PipelineTimeSlot_1\)–\(\PipelineTimeSlot_3\)) and drain (\(\PipelineTimeSlot_{12}\)–\(\PipelineTimeSlot_{14}\)) bubbles still leave each device idle for 6 of 14 time slots.

Tracing the schedule grid in figure 14 column by column reveals how the fill and drain bubbles arise. In the first slot only stage 0 has work, the forward pass of microbatch 1. In the second, stage 0 starts microbatch 2 while stage 1 receives microbatch 1’s activations, and by the fourth slot every stage holds a different microbatch. The last stage then turns microbatch 4 around into its backward pass, and gradients travel upstream in reverse microbatch order, so stage 0 waits until the eleventh slot for its first gradient. Each stage therefore idles 3 slots in the forward phase and 3 more in the backward phase, 6 of the 14 slots in the step. Those idle slots may fall at the start of its row, the middle, or the end. That share, about 43 percent, is the bubble fraction \((\PipelineParallelDegree-1)/(\MicrobatchCount+\PipelineParallelDegree-1)\) at \(\PipelineParallelDegree=\MicrobatchCount=\) 4. Adding microbatches lengthens the busy diagonal without lengthening fill and drain in this balanced schedule. Increasing \(\MicrobatchCount\) is therefore one way to reduce the idle fraction; later schedules also change which work can occupy the gaps.

Enough microbatches shrink the bubble even as stages double.

The same bubble term becomes concrete in the following worked example, where an 8-stage pipeline and 32 microbatches still leave a measurable amount of idle time.

↪ The bubble fraction across stage and microbatch counts, and its interleaved-schedule variant, are developed in Pipeline Arithmetic.

Napkin Math 6.8: The cost of the pipeline bubble
Problem: A large-model training run uses pipeline parallelism across 8 stages. To hide the sequential delay, the batch is split into 32 microbatches. What fraction of GPU cycles does the pipeline bubble leave idle?

Math: For balanced, noninterleaved synchronous stages under the one-forward-one-backward (1F1B) schedule developed below, with ideal transfers, the bubble fraction follows from the stage and microbatch counts. The time units below each combine one forward and one backward service interval; they are not individual optimizer steps.

  1. Wait time: At the start and end of each batch, GPUs sit idle for \(\PipelineParallelDegree-1\) steps.
  2. Productive time: GPUs compute for \(\MicrobatchCount\) steps.
  3. Bubble fraction: \((\PipelineParallelDegree-1) / (\PipelineParallelDegree-1+\MicrobatchCount) =\) 7 \(/\) (7 \(+\) 32) \(\approx\) 17.9 percent.

Systems insight: Pipeline parallelism is a utilization-memory trade-off. With this stage count and schedule fixed, reducing the bubble from 18 percent to 5 percent requires more microbatches \((\MicrobatchCount)\). In general, holding the bubble under 10 percent requires \(\MicrobatchCount> 9(\PipelineParallelDegree-1)\), because \((\PipelineParallelDegree-1)/(\MicrobatchCount+\PipelineParallelDegree-1) < 0.1\) rearranges to that bound. The activation memory those microbatches need depends on the schedule. GPipe retains activations across the microbatches, whereas the 1F1B schedule developed next releases them during the steady state. The modeled 17.9 percent idle fraction also motivates interleaved pipelining, which divides each device’s work into smaller stages. Interleaving can reduce the original 18 percent bubble, but additional transfers and memory requirements determine the net gain.

GPipe4 (Huang et al. 2019) introduced synchronous pipeline parallelism with microbatch accumulation, while PipeDream (Narayanan et al. 2019) developed asynchronous approaches with weight stashing. Modern systems use 1F1B (one-forward-one-backward)5 scheduling to reclaim activation memory earlier. That lower memory pressure makes larger microbatch counts practical, and the larger \(\MicrobatchCount\) is what shrinks the bubble term.

4 GPipe: Published by Google in 2019, GPipe introduced synchronous microbatch pipelining that trained a 557M-parameter AmoebaNet across 8 Tensor Processing Units (TPUs) with near-linear scaling. Its schedule keeps every microbatch’s activations live until the backward pass, the cost that later schedules reduce.

Huang, Y., Y. Cheng, A. Bapna, O. Firat, D. Chen, M. X. Chen, H. Lee, et al. 2019. “GPipe: Efficient Training of Giant Neural Networks Using Pipeline Parallelism.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 32: 103–12.
Narayanan, Deepak, Aaron Harlap, Amar Phanishayee, Vivek Seshadri, Nikhil R. Devanur, Gregory R. Ganger, Phillip B. Gibbons, and Matei Zaharia. 2019. “PipeDream: Generalized Pipeline Parallelism for DNN Training.” Proceedings of the 27th ACM Symposium on Operating Systems Principles, 1–15. https://doi.org/10.1145/3341301.3359646.

5 1F1B (one-forward-one-backward) scheduling: Unlike GPipe (which processes all forward passes before any backward passes), 1F1B interleaves them. For equal-sized microbatch activations and a non-interleaved schedule, peak retained activations per stage fall from \(\mathcal{O}(\MicrobatchCount)\) under GPipe to at most \(\mathcal{O}(\PipelineParallelDegree)\) under 1F1B, where \(\MicrobatchCount\) is the microbatch count and \(\PipelineParallelDegree\) the pipeline-stage count. Summed across stages, the corresponding bounds are \(\mathcal{O}(\MicrobatchCount\PipelineParallelDegree)\) and \(\mathcal{O}(\PipelineParallelDegree^2)\). This memory reclamation is what enables the large microbatch counts \((\MicrobatchCount\gg \PipelineParallelDegree)\) required to keep the pipeline bubble small.

At the schedule level, 1F1B is a warmup, steady-state, and drain loop. In algorithm 1, each stage alternates forward work for newer microbatches with backward work for older microbatches once gradients arrive, so activations can be released before the whole batch has completed.

\(\PipelineparallelDegreePp\)

Run on four stages with eight microbatches, the schedule gives each stage a short warm-up of forward passes, a steady state that alternates one backward with one forward, and a drain of the remaining backward passes (figure 15). The warm-up and drain phases still leave a bubble of roughly \((\PipelineParallelDegree-1)/(\MicrobatchCount+\PipelineParallelDegree-1)\), 6 of 22 slots here. Interleaving backward work in the steady state, however, frees each activation as soon as its backward consumes it. Stage 0 never holds more than four microbatches’ activations where an all-forwards-first schedule would hold all eight, so peak activation memory grows with the stage count rather than the microbatch count. That is the gain over GPipe-style all-forward-then-all-backward scheduling, and it lets the system raise \(\MicrobatchCount\) until activation memory, recomputation, or the global-batch constraint becomes the next limit.

The activation handoff has shape \(\MicrobatchSize \times \SequenceLength\times \HiddenDimension\); its completion still constrains the next stage. The 1F1B schedule in algorithm 1 reduces activation residency, but fill, drain, communication, and load imbalance leave idle time. Filling those remaining slots requires separating work that releases an upstream dependency from work that can wait.

Zero-bubble pipeline schedules further reduce idle time by decoupling the two matrix multiplications inside each layer’s backward pass. In a standard backward pass, the GPU computes both \(\partial \LossFunction/ \partial \LayerInputMatrix\) (the activation gradient propagated backward to the preceding stage) and \(\partial \LossFunction/ \partial \WeightMatrix\) (the weight gradient accumulated locally for the optimizer step) back-to-back as a single backward unit. Zero-bubble scheduling splits them into separate kernels. A B kernel multiplies the incoming upstream gradient by the stage’s persistent weights \(\WeightMatrix^T\) to compute only the activation gradient \(\partial \LossFunction/ \partial \LayerInputMatrix\) and immediately sends it to the previous stage. A W kernel multiplies the saved forward activation \(\LayerInputMatrix^T\) by the upstream gradient to compute the weight gradient \(\partial \LossFunction/ \partial \WeightMatrix\) locally. Because upstream stages cannot begin their own backward work until they receive \(\partial \LossFunction/ \partial \LayerInputMatrix\), the B kernel sits on the critical path and must execute promptly, whereas the W kernel has no cross-stage dependency and can be scheduled opportunistically to fill local pipeline bubbles.

The B/W split creates scheduling freedom, although all deferred W kernels must still finish before the corresponding optimizer update. In the balanced four-stage, eight-microbatch schedule of figure 15, noninterleaved 1F1B still leaves \(3/11 \approx 27\) percent of each stage’s time idle, by the bubble fraction derived above. Reordering forward, activation-gradient, and weight-gradient work allows deferred W kernels to soak up those idle slots. Approaching zero bubble time in practice also depends on kernel durations, memory headroom, and optimizer synchronization, so splitting the backward pass alone does not guarantee it.

The trade-off is memory. Because the B kernel needs only the incoming gradient and the persistent weights \(\WeightMatrix\), whereas the W kernel requires the saved forward activation \(\LayerInputMatrix\), a stage cannot free a microbatch’s forward activation when B finishes. Deferring W to fill a later bubble forces the stage to keep both \(\LayerInputMatrix\) and the incoming gradient resident in device memory until W finally executes, increasing peak memory usage. Some implementations address this by combining zero-bubble scheduling with activation checkpointing, selectively recomputing certain activations before W rather than storing them across the delay. The interaction between these techniques creates a three-way trade-off among pipeline bubble size, memory consumption, and recomputation overhead, an example of the displacement of overhead (principle 14).

6.5.3 Tensor parallelism

Every pipeline schedule so far keeps whole layers on each device, which is why its traffic is confined to stage boundaries and tolerates moderate interconnect bandwidth. The same property sets its limit. A pipeline cannot help when a single layer’s weights or activations exceed one device, and it cannot shorten the time one layer takes, because that layer still runs on one accelerator.

Tensor parallelism splits the weight matrices within each layer instead of assigning complete layers to devices. This operator-level parallelism (also called intra-layer parallelism) enables finer-grained distribution but requires high-bandwidth interconnects for the frequent intra-layer communication it introduces.

Definition 6.9: Tensor parallelism

Tensor parallelism partitions individual tensor operations across devices, commonly through column-parallel or row-parallel weight splits. Communication combines or redistributes the resulting tensor shards as required by the next operation. The Megatron-style forward-pass example below illustrates one such arrangement.

  1. Significance: The Megatron-LM arrangement developed below uses two AllReduce operations per transformer block in the forward pass, one after attention and one after the MLP. Each reduces a 2-byte activation tensor of \(\BatchSize\times \SequenceLength\times \HiddenDimension\) elements, where \(\BatchSize\) counts the sequences in flight through the layer. A ring AllReduce moves roughly \(2(\TensorParallelDegree-1)/\TensorParallelDegree\) times that payload per GPU, and the exchange recurs in every one of the 96 blocks of the GPT-3 shape, with backward adding corresponding exchanges. Whether that traffic hides behind computation depends on the link that carries it, which the arithmetic after the mechanism quantifies. Computing model FLOPs utilization (MFU) requires the complete compute and exposed-communication time, not the bandwidth ratio alone.
  2. Distinction: Unlike pipeline parallelism, which communicates only at layer boundaries and transfers small activation slices, tensor parallelism synchronizes within every layer via AllReduce. It is therefore sensitive to the per-operation latency of the interconnect and favors a fast communication domain such as NVLink, which can extend beyond one node.
  3. Common pitfall: Extending tensor parallelism across nodes without checking the transfer budget can erase the compute saving. The reference NVLink specification is approximately 900 GB/s bidirectional, or 450 GB/s per direction; InfiniBand NDR provides 50 GB/s per port, a 9× per-direction gap. These specifications identify a placement risk. Whether the split helps depends on sustained bandwidth, operation size, compute saved, and overlap.

Frequent intra-layer exchanges favor placing tensor-parallel groups within the fastest available communication domain. Pipeline stages communicate at coarser boundaries and can often span slower domains when their transfers fit the schedule. This difference motivates a hybrid that maps each exchange to a suitable fabric tier; it does not impose a universal node boundary for either strategy.

Tensor-parallel exchanges belong on the fast tier, about 9\(\times\) an NDR port.

6 Megatron-LM: NVIDIA’s 2019 framework that trained an 8.3B-parameter transformer (24× BERT-Large and 5.5× GPT-2 at the time) with only two AllReduce operations per transformer block in the forward pass (one after attention, one after the multilayer perceptron (MLP)). The run reached 76 percent scaling efficiency across 512 GPUs, and its partitioning remains influential in large-scale transformer training.

Shoeybi, Mohammad, Mostofa Patwary, Raul Puri, Patrick LeGresley, Jared Casper, and Bryan Catanzaro. 2019. “Megatron-LM: Training Multi-Billion Parameter Language Models Using Model Parallelism.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1909.08053.

Megatron-style tensor parallelism6 (Shoeybi et al. 2019) partitions matrix multiplications by alternating column and row splits. In the two-GPU dataflow of figure 16, column-parallel splitting partitions the first weight matrix \(\ColumnParallelWeightMatrix= [\ColumnParallelWeightMatrix_1 \mid \ColumnParallelWeightMatrix_2]\) to produce local activation slices \(\LayerOutputMatrix_1, \LayerOutputMatrix_2\) from replicated input \(\LayerInputMatrix\). Row-parallel splitting then partitions the second weight matrix \(\RowParallelWeightMatrix= [\RowParallelWeightMatrix_1 ; \RowParallelWeightMatrix_2]\) so that each GPU computes partial products \(\RowParallelOutputMatrix_1, \RowParallelOutputMatrix_2\) locally. This defers cross-device communication to a single amber AllReduce that reconstructs output \(\RowParallelOutputMatrix\).

Column-parallel linear layers split weights along columns. For input \(\LayerInputMatrix\) and weight matrix \(\WeightMatrix= [\WeightMatrix_1 \mid \WeightMatrix_2]\) split across 2 GPUs: \[\LayerOutputMatrix= \LayerInputMatrix\WeightMatrix= \LayerInputMatrix[\WeightMatrix_1 \mid \WeightMatrix_2] = [\LayerInputMatrix\WeightMatrix_1 \mid \LayerInputMatrix\WeightMatrix_2]\] Each GPU computes its partition independently. The concatenation in the equation is logical, because when the next operation is row-parallel, each output shard remains on its producing device and feeds the matching weight shard without an AllGather. The row-parallel half of the pattern splits weights along rows, \(\WeightMatrix= \begin{bmatrix} \WeightMatrix_1 \\ \WeightMatrix_2 \end{bmatrix}\): \[\LayerOutputMatrix= \LayerInputMatrix\WeightMatrix= \LayerInputMatrix_1 \WeightMatrix_1 + \LayerInputMatrix_2 \WeightMatrix_2\] Each GPU computes a partial sum, and the system pays one AllReduce to combine the partial outputs.

The column-then-row sequence of figure 16 embodies the governing architectural invariant. Pairing a column-parallel layer directly with a row-parallel layer allows intermediate activations (\(\LayerOutputMatrix_1, \LayerOutputMatrix_2\)) to remain strictly local on each GPU, confining inter-device synchronization to a single collective at the end of the block. This locality holds because the intermediate operation between the two projections acts independently on each GPU’s column slice without cross-rank data. Megatron applies that pattern twice in each transformer block. In multi-head attention, the QKV projection is column-parallel along the head dimension so that each GPU receives the complete query, key, and value vectors for a disjoint subset of \(\AttentionHeadCount/\TensorParallelDegree\) attention heads, computes attention locally for those heads, and feeds the resulting head outputs into the row-parallel attention output projection. In the feed-forward network, the first linear layer is column-parallel, the element-wise nonlinearity applies independently to each coordinate of the local slice, and the second linear layer is row-parallel. The forward pass therefore places one AllReduce after attention and one after the feed-forward network, for two forward AllReduce operations per transformer layer. In the backward pass, the dataflow reverses so that gradients enter each row-parallel output layer without communication, while at the input of each column-parallel layer every GPU computes a partial input gradient from its local weight slice and runs an AllReduce to sum those contributions before the preceding residual connection, adding two symmetric backward AllReduces per layer. Communication volume per transformer layer depends on sequence length \(\SequenceLength\), hidden dimension \(\HiddenDimension\), tensor-parallel degree \(\TensorParallelDegree\), and batch size \(\BatchSize\). The batch size is the number of sequences in flight through the layer (the local batch, or the microbatch \(\MicrobatchSize\) when the layer also sits in a pipeline). The activation payload is: \[\ActivationPayloadSize= \BatchSize\times \SequenceLength\times \HiddenDimension\times \ElementStorageSize\]

A ring AllReduce transfers approximately \(2(\TensorParallelDegree-1)/\TensorParallelDegree \times \ActivationPayloadSize\) bytes per GPU, which is close to \(2\ActivationPayloadSize\) for large \(\TensorParallelDegree\). For the GPT-3 shape (\(\SequenceLength=\) 2048, \(\HiddenDimension=\) 12,288, 96 layers) with \(\BatchSize=\) 4 and FP16, the activation payload is 4 \(\times\) 2048 \(\times\) 12,288 \(\times\) 2 \(\approx\) 201 MB.

At \(\TensorParallelDegree=\) 8 the ring factor is 1.75, giving roughly 352 MB per AllReduce. With two AllReduces in each of the 96 layers, this totals approximately 67.6 GB per forward microbatch. The symmetric backward exchanges bring the total to roughly 135 GB per forward/backward microbatch, before any recomputation. Set against the computation it serves, that volume is modest on the right link and prohibitive on the wrong one. At the per-layer compute rate of the earlier feasibility check, one such microbatch takes about 2.2 s of forward and backward computation per GPU. Its tensor-parallel traffic takes about 0.3 s on NVLink but about 2.7 s on one InfiniBand port, longer than the computation. The number of microbatches per update and the available overlap then determine the required fabric service rate.

Tensor parallelism eventually reaches a workload-dependent limit because smaller operation shards leave less computation to cover communication. Per-GPU ring AllReduce bytes approach 2\(\times\) the activation tensor size, while local matrix shapes and collective startup costs change with group size. The useful degree therefore depends on layer shape, microbatch size, and the fast communication domain. Llama 3 405B’s TP=8 arrangement within NVLink-connected H100 nodes (Dubey et al. 2024) and node-local groups in other large training systems (Z. Jiang et al. 2024) illustrate a placement choice, not a universal eight-device limit. Tensor parallelism also costs more to adopt than data parallelism. It replaces standard linear layers with column-parallel and row-parallel variants inside the model definition, whereas data parallelism wraps the model externally without modifying its internals.

Dubey, Abhimanyu, Abhinav Jauhri, Abhinav Pandey, Abhishek Kadian, et al. 2024. The Llama 3 Herd of Models. arXiv preprint arXiv:2407.21783.
Jiang, Ziheng, Haibin Lin, Yinmin Zhong, Qi Huang, Yangrui Chen, Zhi Zhang, Yanghua Peng, et al. 2024. “MegaScale: Scaling Large Language Model Training to More Than 10,000 GPUs.” 21st USENIX Symposium on Networked Systems Design and Implementation (NSDI 24), 745–60.

6.5.4 Sequence and context parallelism

While tensor parallelism partitions weight matrices within linear layers, the memory footprint of large-scale transformer training is increasingly dominated by activations. As models scale their context length from thousands to hundreds of thousands or millions of tokens (\(\SequenceLength\ge 32\text{k}\dots 1\text{M}+\)), the activation footprint scales proportionally: \[\ActivationMemory = \mathcal{O}(\BatchSize\times \SequenceLength\times \HiddenDimension\times \LayerCount)\] for linear, normalization, and residual layers, and \(\mathcal{O}(\BatchSize\times \AttentionHeadCount \times \SequenceLength^2 \times \LayerCount)\) for un-tiled attention (or \(\mathcal{O}(\BatchSize\times \SequenceLength\times \HiddenDimension\times \LayerCount)\) with tiled FlashAttention). At long contexts, activation memory dwarfs parameter storage, exposing the memory capacity gap (principle 2). Two complementary methods address this constraint by sharding along the sequence axis: sequence parallelism (SP) within the tensor-parallel domain, and context parallelism (CP) across dedicated long-sequence process groups.

Definition 6.10: Sequence and context parallelism

Sequence and context parallelism partition the token sequence dimension to reduce per-device activation storage. Sequence parallelism (SP) retains sequence shards during operations such as LayerNorm and Dropout within a tensor-parallel group. Context parallelism (CP) partitions the sequence across a dedicated group and exchanges the information needed for attention, through mechanisms developed below that either pass key/value blocks around a ring or transpose between sequence and head partitions.

  1. Significance: Long contexts can exhaust activation memory even when sharded model state fits. The relevant limit depends on batch size, layer shapes, attention kernels, and checkpointing. Sequence parallelism divides the targeted activation storage by \(\TensorParallelDegree\) while preserving the ideal collective payload volume in the decomposition below. Context parallelism distributes sequence-dependent storage; linear context growth (\(\ContextParallelDegree\times \SingleDeviceContextLength\), with \(\ContextParallelDegree\) denoting the context-parallel degree) is an ideal capacity estimate that still requires communication buffers and computation to fit the budget.
  2. Distinction: Tensor parallelism partitions weight matrices along hidden dimensions \(\HiddenDimension\), whereas context parallelism partitions the sequence axis \(\SequenceLength\). Ring Attention exchanges key/value chunks between devices, while Ulysses uses an AllToAll to transpose between sequence and head dimensions. Both require placement that can service their exchanges within the attention schedule.
  3. Common pitfall: Assuming context parallelism eliminates the quadratic attention compute complexity. CP distributes the attention computation across devices, reducing per-device memory and FLOPs by \(1/\ContextParallelDegree\), but the aggregate cluster FLOPs for dense self-attention still scale quadratically as \(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength^2)\).

Megatron sequence parallelism

In the Megatron-style arrangement just derived, the column-then-row pairing in figure 16 splits the QKV projection and first MLP layer column-wise, and the attention output projection and second MLP layer row-wise. The surrounding LayerNorm, Dropout, and residual operations retain replicated activations across all \(\TensorParallelDegree\) devices. LayerNorm operates across a token’s hidden features, while Dropout and residual addition operate element-wise; none requires another token’s features. This token independence permits partitioning the otherwise replicated \(\BatchSize\times \SequenceLength\times \HiddenDimension\) activation tensor along its sequence dimension.

Megatron Sequence Parallelism (Korthikanti et al. 2023) eliminates this redundancy by sharding the sequence dimension across the \(\TensorParallelDegree\) ranks for all non-tensor-parallel operations. Instead of replicating activations, each GPU holds only a \(\BatchSize\times (\SequenceLength/\TensorParallelDegree) \times \HiddenDimension\) slice during LayerNorm and Dropout.

Korthikanti, Vijay Anand, Jared Casper, Sangkug Lym, Lawrence McAfee, Michael Andersch, Mohammad Shoeybi, and Bryan Catanzaro. 2023. “Reducing Activation Recomputation in Large Transformer Models.” Proceedings of Machine Learning and Systems (MLSys) 5: 341–53.

This sharding incurs zero additional communication volume. In standard tensor parallelism, the row-parallel matrix multiplication is followed by an AllReduce to sum partial products across ranks. An AllReduce decomposes into a ReduceScatter followed by an AllGather, the identity established among the collective primitives in The six core primitives: \[\text{AllReduce}(\RankContribution) \equiv \text{AllGather}(\text{ReduceScatter}(\RankContribution))\] Megatron-SP decouples this collective pair across the transformer block. Following the row-parallel linear layer, a ReduceScatter reduces partial sums while scattering the resulting activations along the sequence dimension, leaving each GPU with an \(\SequenceLength/\TensorParallelDegree\) token slice. LayerNorm, residual additions, and Dropout then execute on these local slices with zero cross-device communication. Immediately before the next column-parallel linear layer, an AllGather across the sequence dimension reassembles the complete \(\SequenceLength\) tokens required for the weight-partitioned matrix multiplication.

In this ideal payload accounting, standard TP executes two AllReduces per transformer block (\(2 \times 2(\TensorParallelDegree-1)/\TensorParallelDegree \times \ActivationPayloadSize\) bytes), while Megatron-SP executes two ReduceScatter and AllGather pairs (\(2 \times [(\TensorParallelDegree-1)/\TensorParallelDegree + (\TensorParallelDegree-1)/\TensorParallelDegree] \times \ActivationPayloadSize= 2 \times 2(\TensorParallelDegree-1)/\TensorParallelDegree \times \ActivationPayloadSize\) bytes).
