The volumes match while the targeted activation storage falls by a factor of \(\TensorParallelDegree\). Equal bytes do not imply equal elapsed time, because separating the collectives changes readiness, startup costs, and overlap opportunities.

Context parallelism with ring attention versus Ulysses

Megatron-SP shards the \(\SequenceLength\) dimension across the \(\TensorParallelDegree\) devices only outside the attention and MLP blocks; inside self-attention, each tensor-parallel rank still processes the full sequence length \(\SequenceLength\) for its local heads, so long-context runs can still exhaust HBM even after sequence parallelism and activation checkpointing. Context parallelism of degree \(\ContextParallelDegree\) breaks that ceiling by partitioning the sequence itself across \(\ContextParallelDegree\) accelerators throughout the entire block, including attention. Because every query token must still attend to keys and values from other sequence partitions, context parallelism requires cross-partition exchanges, supplied either by ring-based point-to-point transfers, by AllToAll head-sequence transpositions, or by a hierarchical combination of both. The ring method relies on an online softmax that maintains a running maximum and running sum of attention scores as key/value tiles arrive, rescaling earlier partial sums whenever the maximum changes so no device ever materializes the full attention matrix.

Ring Attention (Liu et al. 2023) organizes devices in a logical ring where each GPU holds a query chunk \(\AttentionQueryMatrix_i\) of length \(\SequenceLength/\ContextParallelDegree\) and an initial key/value chunk \((\AttentionKeyMatrix_i, \AttentionValuesMatrix_i)\). Computation proceeds in \(\ContextParallelDegree\) rounds along the ring. In each round, GPU \(\ContextParallelRankIndex\) computes block attention between its stationary \(\AttentionQueryMatrix_i\) and resident \((\AttentionKeyMatrix_j, \AttentionValuesMatrix_j)\) tiles using an online softmax accumulator. At the same time, it asynchronously transmits \((\AttentionKeyMatrix_j, \AttentionValuesMatrix_j)\) to ring neighbor \((\ContextParallelRankIndex+1) \bmod \ContextParallelDegree\) and receives \((\AttentionKeyMatrix_{j-1}, \AttentionValuesMatrix_{j-1})\) from \((\ContextParallelRankIndex-1) \bmod \ContextParallelDegree\). After \(\ContextParallelDegree\) rounds every query chunk has attended to every key/value tile, yet no device ever stores the full key/value sequence (figure 17).

Liu, Hao, Matei Zaharia, and Pieter Abbeel. 2023. “Ring Attention with Blockwise Transformers for Near-Infinite Context.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2310.01889, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2310.01889.

For one sequence with standard multi-head attention, let \(\ContextChunkTokenCount=\SequenceLength/\ContextParallelDegree\) be the tokens per chunk, \(\HiddenDimension\) the total head width, \(\ElementStorageSize\) the bytes per stored element, \(\PeakRate\EfficiencyHw\) the sustained attention FLOP/s, and \(\SingleLinkBandwidth\) the one-way transfer rate. Computing both \(\AttentionQueryMatrix\AttentionKeyMatrix^T\) and the weighted-value product costs approximately \(4\ContextChunkTokenCount^2 \HiddenDimension\) FLOPs, while sending \(\AttentionKeyMatrix\) and \(\AttentionValuesMatrix\) costs \(2 \ContextChunkTokenCount \HiddenDimension\, \ElementStorageSize\) bytes. Ignoring startup, softmax, and overlap interference gives the necessary tile-size estimate: \[\frac{4\ContextChunkTokenCount^2 \HiddenDimension}{\PeakRate\EfficiencyHw} \ge \frac{2 \ContextChunkTokenCount \HiddenDimension\, \ElementStorageSize}{\SingleLinkBandwidth} \iff \ContextChunkTokenCount\ge \frac{\ElementStorageSize \PeakRate\EfficiencyHw}{2\SingleLinkBandwidth}\] This compares computation time with transfer time; HBM bandwidth cannot substitute for \(\PeakRate\EfficiencyHw\). It is an idealized overlap condition, not a guarantee.

For an H100 sustaining half its dense FP16 peak with 2-byte elements, the condition requires chunks of at least about 1,100 tokens per device when KV tiles travel over NVLink, and about 9,900 tokens when they cross one NDR InfiniBand port. A million-token context split across a few dozen devices clears both bounds, whereas splitting a 32K context across many nodes can leave each chunk too short to hide its transfers.

Grouped-query attention changes the transferred KV width, and causal masking changes the tile work. Both require recomputing the two costs. In causal autoregressive attention, a query token can attend only to earlier or current positions, so whenever a rank holding query chunk \(\ContextParallelRankIndex\) receives a key and value chunk from a later sequence position \(\KvTileIndex> \ContextParallelRankIndex\), the entire upper-triangular tile is masked out. Under naive contiguous partitioning, rank \(\ContextParallelRankIndex = 0\) holds the earliest tokens and performs useful work only on its own chunk (\(\KvTileIndex = 0\)), sitting idle during the remaining \(\ContextParallelDegree - 1\) ring rounds while rank \(\ContextParallelDegree - 1\) (holding the final tokens) computes on every round. Because the ring steps synchronously at the pace of the busiest rank, this load imbalance leaves half the cluster’s compute cycles idle. Production implementations eliminate this skew with zigzag or striped token partitioning, which splits the sequence into \(2\ContextParallelDegree\) sub-chunks and gives each device one early sub-chunk and one complementary late sub-chunk (for example, one chunk from the start of the sequence and one from the end). Every device then holds the same share of unmasked causal tiles in every round of the ring.

DeepSpeed Ulysses (Jacobs et al. 2023) exploits the independence of attention heads rather than circulating tiles along a ring. Each head attends over the whole sequence without reading any other head’s scores, so the attention work can be split by heads instead of by tokens. Outside the attention core, activations are sharded along the sequence dimension (\(\SequenceLength/\ContextParallelDegree\) tokens, all \(\AttentionHeadCount\) attention heads). Before computing self-attention, an AllToAll collective transposes the tensor from sequence-partitioned [S/c, h, d_head] to head-partitioned [S, h/c, d_head]. Each GPU now holds the full sequence length \(\SequenceLength\), but only a subset \(\AttentionHeadCount/\ContextParallelDegree\) of attention heads. Each device computes standard local FlashAttention across the full sequence for its heads; each rank owns complete heads, while the kernel handles causal masking locally. A second AllToAll collective transposes the attention output back to [S/c, h, d_head]. Ulysses is conceptually clean and reuses single-GPU attention kernels, but requires that the head count be divisible by the context degree (\(\AttentionHeadCount \ge \ContextParallelDegree\)). Under Grouped Query Attention (GQA), where the key-value head count \(\KvHeadCount\) is smaller, a direct head-sharding implementation requires compatible divisibility and \(\ContextParallelDegree\le \KvHeadCount\). Replicating KV heads changes that limit and its communication budget.

Jacobs, Sam Ade, Masahiro Tanaka, Chengming Zhang, Minjia Guo, Shuaiwen Zhu, Shuai Song, Samyam Rajbhandari, Ammar Ahmad Awan, Connor Zheng, and Yuxiong He. 2023. “DeepSpeed Ulysses: System Optimizations for Enabling Training of Extreme Long Sequence Transformer Models.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2309.14509.

The ownership map in figure 18 makes this transpose explicit. Unlike the stationary query chunks in figure 17, a Ulysses rank receives every token for its assigned heads.

The exchanged tiles change where attention executes rather than removing its interactions. Head count and KV-head layout constrain this partition even when the activation bytes fit in memory.

A hierarchical configuration can combine head-sequence transposes within a fast domain and ring exchanges between domains. For an eight-device intra-node domain, a Ulysses group satisfies (\(\IntraNodeContextParallelDegree\le 8\)), while a second degree (\(\InterNodeContextParallelDegree\)) determines the ring’s inter-node partition. In this example, Ulysses partitions heads locally and Ring Attention circulates KV blocks across nodes. The arrangement is feasible only if head divisibility, peak memory, link service time, and the overlap condition all hold; hierarchy alone does not prevent fabric saturation.

6.5.5 Expert parallelism (mixture of experts)

Tensor, pipeline, sequence, and context parallelism all partition dense layers that every token traverses, so scaling a dense model’s parameter count increases its per-token compute cost in lockstep. MoE architectures (Shazeer et al. 2017) break that lockstep through conditional computation, and expert parallelism distributes the resulting capacity across devices. In an MoE layer, the feed-forward block contains \(\ExpertCount\) independent feed-forward networks (“experts”), and a gating network selects only a small subset, often top-1 or top-2, for each token. Adding more experts while holding each expert’s shape and the selected count fixed expands stored parameters without increasing expert FLOPs per token, trading dense matrix work for routing and all-to-all communication.

Shazeer, Noam, Azalia Mirhoseini, Krzysztof Maziarz, Andy Davis, Quoc Le, Geoffrey Hinton, and Jeff Dean. 2017. “Outrageously Large Neural Networks: The Sparsely-Gated Mixture-of-Experts Layer.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

In a distributed setting, experts are partitioned across workers, with each rank hosting a disjoint subset of expert networks. One routing cycle across four ranks shows the sequence (figure 19). After top-\(\RoutedExpertCount\) gating selects destination experts, an AllToAll dispatch shuffles tokens across the fabric to the hosting ranks. Expert FFNs then compute locally, and a second AllToAll return routes the processed activations back to the originating ranks to form the gate-weighted sum. The backward pass repeats both exchanges in reverse order, sending output gradients to the ranks hosting the experts and returning input gradients to the ranks that own the tokens. The primary advantage is decoupling model size from per-token compute, which lets parameter count grow within feasible hardware budgets. The primary constraint is the AllToAll communication, which is bandwidth-intensive and sensitive to load imbalance.

Definition 6.11: Expert parallelism

Expert parallelism is the distribution strategy for mixture-of-experts models that places different experts on different devices and routes each token’s activations to the devices hosting its selected experts through AllToAll collectives, scaling total parameter count across the cluster without scaling the compute each token consumes.

  1. Significance: It decouples model capacity from compute budget. For illustration, a trillion-parameter MoE model may activate on the order of 10B parameters per token, so per-token FLOPs remain near those of a 10B dense model while capacity grows roughly 100\(\times\). The price is communication structure: every MoE layer requires two AllToAll shuffles per pass (dispatch and combine) whose volume scales with tokens moved times hidden dimension, stressing the cluster’s bisection bandwidth rather than any single link.
  2. Distinction: Unlike tensor parallelism, which splits dense operations so that every device computes a fraction of every token, expert parallelism is conditional computation. Each device computes only the tokens routed to its experts, which makes both the communication pattern and the load distribution data-dependent rather than fixed by the architecture.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that experts share work evenly. A hot expert receiving, for example, 3–5\(\times\) its uniform share can overflow its device’s activation buffer while sibling devices idle. Capacity limits and routing penalties are possible responses; a design that retains every assignment must instead accommodate the resulting memory and scheduling demand.

↩︎ The terms that set an AllToAll’s completion time, from traffic across the fabric bisection to hotspots created by skewed expert loads, are established in AllToAll Exchanges at Scale.

Fedus, William, Barret Zoph, and Noam Shazeer. 2022. “Switch Transformers: Scaling to Trillion Parameter Models with Simple and Efficient Sparsity.” Journal of Machine Learning Research 23 (120): 1–39.
Lepikhin, Dmitry, HyoukJoong Lee, Yuanzhong Xu, Dehao Chen, Orhan Firat, Yanping Huang, Maxim Krikun, Noam Shazeer, and Zhifeng Chen. 2021. “GShard: Scaling Giant Models with Conditional Computation and Automatic Sharding.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

AllToAll dispatch routes token activations to their assigned experts. Consider \(\ExpertCount=\) 64 experts across 64 GPUs, processing a routing-group batch of \(\BatchSize=\) 4 sequences of length \(\SequenceLength=\) 2048 and hidden dimension \(\HiddenDimension=\) 4096. Under top-1 routing, \(\BatchSize\times \SequenceLength\) tokens each contribute one activation vector. At FP16, the logical dispatch payload is \(\BatchSize\cdot \SequenceLength\cdot \HiddenDimension\cdot 2\) bytes, approximately 67 MB; returning expert outputs doubles the forward payload to about 134 MB per block. Local destinations reduce bytes crossing the network, multiple selected experts increase routes, and backward adds gradient transfers. The resulting service time depends on placement and load balance, including in Switch Transformer (Fedus et al. 2022) (up to 2,048 experts) and GShard (Lepikhin et al. 2021).

Uniform routing is a useful capacity baseline, not a guarantee. An expert receiving several times its uniform share, the hot expert of the definition above, needs correspondingly more token processing and activation storage. The actual skew depends on the learned router and batch. A hot expert can exhaust its buffer or delay the step while other experts remain underused, coupling memory feasibility to load balance.

One response bounds expert workload with a capacity factor \(\ExpertCapacityFactor\). For top-1 routing, a capacity of roughly \(\ExpertCapacityFactor\cdot (\BatchSize\times \SequenceLength)/\ExpertCount\) token assignments per expert provides headroom above uniform load. A dropping implementation sends excess tokens past that expert through the residual connection; implementations that retain every assignment instead need memory and scheduling to accommodate the imbalance. An auxiliary load balancing loss can encourage less concentrated routing. Models using more than one expert per token, such as Mixtral 8x7B with top-2 routing (A. Q. Jiang et al. 2024), require a capacity budget that counts all selected assignments rather than reusing the top-1 denominator unchanged.

Jiang, A. Q., A. Sablayrolles, A. Roux, A. Mensch, B. Savary, C. Bamford, D. S. Chaplot, et al. 2024. “Mixtral of Experts.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2401.04088.

Sparse routing gives MoE layers the same coordination problem that the embedding tables of Archetype B (DLRM at Scale) pose. In both, which device a token’s data must reach depends on the data itself, so load balance cannot be fixed in advance. MoE language models nonetheless remain Archetype A (GPT-4/Llama-3) workloads, because their dense layers still carry the tensor, pipeline, and data-parallel traffic developed above.

6.5.6 Sparse embedding sharding

Expert parallelism places parameters that only some tokens touch. Recommendation models push that sparsity much further. Their parameters are dominated by embedding tables (often 10 TB+) that no single GPU can hold, and their updates are sparse, because a batch of users interacts with only a tiny fraction of the items. Sharding those tables is model parallelism over stored state rather than over computation. The parameter server (PS) architecture, formalized by Li et al. (2014), remains a common way to do it. A parameter server architecture separates workers, which compute gradients, from servers, which store parameters and apply updates.

For dense models such as ResNet or BERT, that separation concentrates traffic, as section 6.3.1.3 noted. With \(\DeviceCount\) workers, a server’s inbound links must absorb \(\DeviceCount\) gradient streams at once. This incast problem is why dense training moved to ring AllReduce, in which every worker sends and receives at its own link rate and the aggregate load spreads across all nodes.

Sparse embedding tables reverse that balance. In a sparse parameter-server design, the tables are sharded across servers, and workers request only rows indexed by their local minibatches. A sparse pull returns those embedding vectors; a sparse push sends updates for the touched rows. This pattern avoids exchanging the complete table, but hot rows, lookup rates, and network contention can still limit throughput. Embedding-sharding frameworks such as TorchRec also support accelerator-based layouts, so sparse partitioning should not be equated with a CPU-only server architecture. Recommendation training at this scale is the volume’s Archetype B (DLRM at Scale) regime, and it differs from the dense running example in which resource binds first.

Whether the shards live on servers or in accelerator memory, each table must be split along some axis. Row-wise sharding partitions embedding tables by row, placing the whole embedding vector of the entity with identifier id on the shard that equation 3 selects:

\[\ShardIndex = \text{hash}(\text{id}) \bmod \EmbeddingShardCount \tag{3}\]

Each shard \(\ShardIndex\) contains approximately \(\EntityCount/\EmbeddingShardCount\) embeddings, where \(\EntityCount\) is the total number of entities and \(\EmbeddingShardCount\) is the shard count. Column-wise sharding instead partitions each embedding vector \(\EntityEmbedding\) across devices, as equation 4 writes it:

\[\EntityEmbedding = [\EntityEmbedding^{(0)}, \EntityEmbedding^{(1)}, \ldots, \EntityEmbedding^{(\EmbeddingShardCount-1)}] \tag{4}\]

Each device stores a slice of every embedding. Hybrid sharding combines both approaches, and the choice among the three depends on lookup patterns and communication overhead (table 6).

The trade-off is locality against load balance. Row-wise sharding keeps each embedding vector whole on one device, while column-wise sharding spreads its dimensions across devices. Both require returning the pieces each consuming computation needs, but placement alone does not prescribe a collective; the runtime can use shard RPCs or collectives according to where inputs and outputs reside. A row-wise layout commonly realizes its lookups as an AllToAll of whole vectors, and a column-wise layout as an AllGather of slices of each vector (figure 20). Hybrid placement can replicate or column-partition hot embeddings while row-sharding the cold tail, trading additional storage and communication against concentrated lookup load.

On accelerators, a row-wise layout turns each training step’s lookups into an AllToAll exchange. Every worker sends the indices its minibatch touches to the shards that own them and receives the embedding vectors back, and the backward pass returns gradients for the touched rows along the same routes. The exchange obeys the AllToAll bound of AllToAll Exchanges at Scale, and like MoE dispatch its volume and balance depend on which rows the data touches, so hot rows overload their owners unless the layout replicates or splits them.

Lighthouse 6.1: Archetype B (DLRM at Scale): DLRM vs. LLM scaling
Each workload scales through a distinct mechanism and is bound by a different resource. Dense LLMs scale through data, tensor, and pipeline parallelism, constrained by compute throughput and by the bandwidth that dense gradient and activation collectives need over fabrics such as NVLink and InfiniBand. Sparse recommendation models such as DLRM scale by sharding embedding tables of 10 TB or more across parameter servers or accelerator memory, where aggregate memory capacity, lookup rate, and the latency of many small random accesses dominate. The distinction shapes cluster design, favoring accelerator pods built around high-bandwidth dense fabrics for LLMs and memory-capacity-rich layouts for recommendation systems. This holds whether the embedding shards live in CPU-hosted servers, in accelerator HBM, or in a tiered hybrid that keeps hot rows on accelerators and cold rows in host memory.

Self-Check: Question

  1. In Megatron-LM style tensor parallelism for a transformer layer, how are the linear projections in the multi-head attention block arranged to minimize cross-device collective communication?

    1. Both QKV and Output projections are column-parallel, requiring an AllGather after each projection
    2. Both QKV and Output projections are row-parallel, requiring an AllReduce before each projection
    3. The QKV projection is column-parallel and the Output projection is row-parallel, requiring only a single AllReduce at the end of the attention block
    4. The QKV projection is partitioned across pipeline stages while the Output projection is replicated across data-parallel ranks

  2. In a synchronous 1F1B pipeline-parallel training schedule with \(p=8\) stages and \(m=32\) microbatches, calculate the theoretical pipeline bubble fraction and explain how increasing the microbatch count \(m\) affects bubble overhead and activation memory.

  3. Order the four sequential phases of token routing and execution in Expert Parallelism (Mixture of Experts) across distributed workers:

  1. AllToAll Combine: Processed token embeddings are routed back to their original source devices for residual connections
  2. Computation: Sharded expert feed-forward networks process their assigned token batches
  3. Gating: A router network evaluates tokens and selects top-k destination experts
  4. AllToAll Dispatch: Tokens are shuffled across the network fabric to the devices hosting their selected experts

  1. In a capacity-limited Mixture of Experts (MoE) system using top-1 routing and token dropping, what occurs when a hot expert receives more tokens than its allocated buffer capacity determined by the Capacity Factor \(C\)?

    1. Excess tokens are dropped and pass through the MoE layer unprocessed via the residual connection
    2. The expert GPU dynamically allocates host CPU memory over PCIe, halting computation on other ranks
    3. The AllToAll collective automatically pauses until all sibling experts process matching token counts
    4. The extra tokens are rerouted to a random cold expert regardless of gating network weights

  2. Contrast the communication patterns and hardware interconnect requirements of dense LLM training (Megatron-style Tensor Parallelism) with large-scale Recommendation Systems (DLRM embedding sharding).

  3. True or False: Ring Attention scales context length beyond single-GPU memory capacity by circulating key/value blocks in an accelerator ring across \(N-1\) communication rounds while overlapping transfer with block-level attention computation.

6.6 Hybrid Parallelism

The 175B-parameter running example has now exhausted each parallelism family on its own. Data parallelism with ZeRO-3 makes the state fit, but the per-step parameter regather priced in section 6.5 would take about 10× as long as the computation it serves, while tensor parallelism confined to one 8-GPU node still leaves several hundred gigabytes of training state per device. Because replication and state sharding (section 6.3) distribute examples and storage whereas tensor and pipeline partitioning (section 6.5) distribute layer computation, hybrid parallelism composes both families orthogonally: model parallelism splits the architecture so each replica fits available HBM and stays on fast links, while data parallelism scales throughput across multiple model replicas to process 300 billion tokens within calendar limits. This orthogonal composition defines Archetype A training at frontier scale.

Lighthouse 6.2: Archetype A (GPT-4/Llama-3): Physics of 3D parallelism
Archetype A (GPT-4/Llama-3) illustrates the motivation for hybrid parallelism. Parameter count \((\Parameters)\) must first be converted to a complete memory budget before comparison with device capacity \((\DeviceMemoryCapacity)\), while dataset size \((\DatasetSize)\) and the deadline determine the throughput target. Tensor parallelism partitions layer computations within a suitable communication domain; pipeline parallelism assigns consecutive layers to stages; and data parallelism distributes examples from \(\DatasetSize\) across replicas. A design combines the dimensions that address its constraints; efficient execution does not require all three in every case.

To see why the combination matters, consider three parallelism configurations for training the 175B model on a 1,024-GPU cluster organized as 128 nodes of 8 GPUs each. The progression from pure data parallelism through tensor and pipeline parallelism makes the efficiency gain concrete.

In Configuration A, pure data parallelism (DP-1024) serves as a communication counterfactual rather than a feasible placement for this model. The FP16 weights alone occupy 350 GB, so an A100 or H100 cannot replicate them; sharding only optimizer states does not change that fact. If replication were possible, all 1,024 ranks would form one data-parallel group, and each rank would participate in an AllReduce of the 350 GB gradient tensor across the InfiniBand fabric. The 15 percent calculation in section 6.4 isolates that communication cost.

Configuration B introduces intra-node tensor parallelism alongside data parallelism (TP-8, DP-128). Within each of 128 model replicas, 8 GPUs use tensor parallelism over NVLink. The corresponding tensor shard from every replica forms one 128-rank data-parallel group, so the layout contains 8 such groups. Each rank AllReduces its 1/8 gradient shard, 43.75 GB rather than 350 GB, and TP communication remains within the fast NVLink domain. This is a communication comparison only. At 16 bytes per parameter each GPU would still hold about 350 GB of training state, so the layout also needs optimizer and gradient sharding across its data-parallel group before it fits. The inter-node AllReduce time drops from 14 seconds to approximately 1.75 seconds. If the compute time is still 2.1 seconds, the unoverlapped efficiency improves to about 54.5 percent. The smaller transfer also fits inside the compute window. The same optimistic schedule assumed for the data-parallel baseline can therefore now hide its full 75 percent of the transfer behind backward computation, which lifts efficiency to about 83 percent.

Configuration C adds pipeline stages across nodes (TP-8, PP-4, DP-32) so that each of 32 model replicas uses 8-way tensor parallelism within a stage and 4 pipeline stages across nodes. For each of the 8 \(\times\) 4 tensor-and-pipeline shard positions, the corresponding ranks from all replicas form one 32-rank data-parallel group. Each rank therefore AllReduces a \(350/(8\times4)\approx10.94\) GB gradient shard. Pipeline communication forwards activations between stages and has lower volume than a full-model AllReduce. The trade-off is the pipeline bubble. At the beginning and end of each training step, some pipeline stages are idle while waiting for activations from earlier stages or gradients from later stages. The bubble fraction is approximately \((\PipelineParallelDegree-1)/(\MicrobatchCount+\PipelineParallelDegree-1)\), so with 4 pipeline stages and 32 microbatches per training step, the bubble wastes roughly 9 percent of compute (\(3/35 \approx 8.6\%\)). The smaller shard takes about 0.44 seconds to AllReduce, and under the same overlap model as configuration B most of it hides behind backward computation. Charging the bubble against the compute window and adding the remaining exposed transfer gives a predicted efficiency of about 87 percent, a few points above configuration B’s 83 percent. The pipeline cuts the data-parallel traffic by another factor of 4, and that saving slightly outweighs the bubble it introduces. Configuration C does not fit memory by itself either. Its training state is about 87.5 GB per GPU, above the 80 GB of an H100. Adding ZeRO-1 across its 32-rank data-parallel group closes that gap. Each GPU then keeps its tensor-and-pipeline slice of weights and gradients but only a 1/32 share of that slice’s optimizer state, about 24 GB in all, which leaves most of the HBM for activations and buffers. ZeRO-1 matches plain DDP’s ideal data-parallel byte volume at matching communication widths (table 3), but equal bytes do not preserve the overlap schedule. Gradient reduction to owners must precede their optimizer updates, and the updated-parameter AllGather follows those updates. The predicted efficiency remains conditional on that schedule achieving the modeled exposed communication time. TP-8, PP-4, DP-32 with ZeRO-1 passes this simplified persistent-state memory budget; its reduction, update, and parameter-gather readiness must still be priced before accepting the throughput prediction.

Within this comparison’s communication model, the configuration that places frequent tensor-parallel exchanges in the fast NVLink domain, pipelines across nodes, and leaves only small data-parallel gradient shards for InfiniBand has the best predicted efficiency. Peak memory, kernel shape, and scheduling still need verification before that prediction becomes a runnable design. The infrastructure hierarchy therefore dictates the parallelism hierarchy. Here, hierarchy-aware parallelism means matching exchange requirements to measured communication domains rather than choosing each degree independently.

Definition 6.12: Hierarchy-aware parallelism

Hierarchy-aware parallelism is the strategy of mapping different parallel execution modes to the physical bandwidth tiers of the cluster.

  1. Significance: It places high-frequency synchronization (for example, tensor parallelism) on fast links such as NVLink, while assigning lower-frequency exchanges (for example, data parallelism) to slower tiers when their transfers fit the schedule. This alignment can improve scaling efficiency \((\ScalingEfficiency)\) by reducing exposed communication time \((\CommunicationTime)\) and latency \((\Latency)\); the gain depends on readiness, contention, and available overlap.
  2. Distinction: Unlike uniform parallelism, which treats all node-to-node links as equal, hierarchy-aware strategies respect the bandwidth cliffs between die, node, and rack boundaries.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that any model can be sharded across any number of nodes. In reality, if the hierarchy mapping is wrong (for example, sharding a large tensor across a slow inter-rack link), the communication time will dwarf the compute time, making the scale-out useless.

The dependence runs both ways, because a parallelism configuration also determines which traffic the fabric must carry. In the TP-8, PP-4, DP-32 layout, tensor reductions stay within each fast local domain, pipeline traffic crosses neighboring stages, and data-parallel groups synchronize corresponding shards across replicas. Rail-optimized and hierarchical fabrics can exploit that structure, but a workload mix that adds expert AllToAll traffic changes the global bandwidth requirement. Infrastructure planning must therefore evaluate the anticipated communication groups together rather than optimizing the fabric for a single layout.

↩︎ Rail-optimized fabrics, which cable each GPU slot position to its own rail switch, are established in Rail-optimized topology.

6.6.1 The 3D training loop

A 175B-parameter model trained with 3D parallelism can be traced on a fleet configured with tensor parallelism (TP) of 8 GPUs, pipeline parallelism (PP) of 16, and data parallelism (DP) of 128. The term names the coordinated composition of data, pipeline, and tensor parallelism introduced with figure 2. This configuration uses 16,384 GPUs (8 GPUs \(\times\) 16 \(\times\) 128) organized into a hierarchy of bandwidth domains. That is sixteen times the 1,024-GPU reference cluster, so every level of the hierarchy is exercised even though parameter count alone would not require all three dimensions.

The training step begins at the data-parallel level. Each of the 128 model replicas receives a distinct slice of the global batch. Within each replica, the model is split across 16 pipeline stages (nodes), with microbatches flowing sequentially from the embedding layer on Node 0 to the loss calculation on Node 15. At the finest granularity, within each node, the 8 GPUs fuse into a single “super-accelerator” via TP. The transformer matrix multiplications are split across these devices; column- and row-parallel pairs retain local intermediate shards and exchange partial results at the reduction points already derived. For a 175B-scale hidden dimension, the activation payload is roughly 201.3 MB. The resulting tensor-parallel traffic is about 4.2 GB per pipeline stage per microbatch in the forward pass, or 8.5 GB including the backward pass. Across the full 96-layer replica, the forward tensor-parallel traffic is about 67.6 GB. This traffic is the highest-intensity in the system, but its exposed latency can remain small relative to compute because the chips communicate over 300 GB/s–450 GB/s of local bandwidth per direction.

The backward pass exposes dependencies between parallelism dimensions. Nodes exchange activation gradients point-to-point, roughly 201.3 MB per microbatch per stage boundary in this example. A smaller payload still stalls a stage if it arrives after the stage needs it. Data-parallel synchronization creates another dependency. Although full-model gradients occupy about 350 GB, each data-parallel group exchanges only the corresponding tensor- and pipeline-parallel parameter shard. Gradient bucketing groups ready gradients as backward computation proceeds. Which exchange limits the step depends on its readiness, service time, and overlap; data parallelism is not necessarily the final bottleneck.

↩︎ Bucket fusion, which groups gradients that become ready together into larger reductions so communication can begin during backpropagation, is established in Bucket fusion.

The architectural imperative is bandwidth matching, in which each exchange’s startup and transfer time must fit its available compute or overlap interval. In this mapping, frequent TP exchanges use the NVLink domain (300 GB/s per direction or higher), while PP transfers cross nodes and DP reductions overlap backward computation. Accumulation can enlarge the compute interval between DP synchronizations, subject to the global-batch constraint. An infeasible mapping leaves part or all of the 16,384 GPUs fleet waiting. Comparing service time with the schedule completes the feasibility check introduced in section 6.2.1.

6.6.2 Hybrid-parallelism worked example

Applied to physical infrastructure, bandwidth matching turns cluster design into a placement problem, and memory capacity per device (80 GB–80 GB) bounds every placement. For the 3D training loop’s layout on 16,384 GPUs, built from 8-GPU NVLink nodes, this reasoning leads to a concrete placement. Fix tensor parallelism at \(\TensorParallelDegree=8\) so the bandwidth-heavy matrix-multiplication collectives stay within one 8-GPU node. Map pipeline parallelism across nodes in the same high-bandwidth rack or island, here \(\PipelineParallelDegree=16\), with \(\PipelineParallelDegree=8\) as the alternative when the per-device memory budget allows. Use data parallelism for the remaining scale-out dimension across pods. The result is not a generic rule but a consequence of matching each traffic pattern to the cheapest fabric tier that can carry it. With the placement fixed, the next question is whether the static model state fits inside each accelerator’s HBM budget.

The memory budget for a 175B model must include every persistent training component. Under the mixed-precision Adam convention used here, each parameter has FP16 weights and gradients, an FP32 master weight, and two FP32 optimizer moments, totaling 16 bytes before activations and workspaces. With tensor parallelism degree 8 alone, these components occupy 350 GB per device, exceeding H100-class capacity. Adding 16 pipeline stages spreads that state across the product of the two degrees, yielding 21.9 GB per device under even partitioning. A budget that counts only weights and optimizer moments, about 13.7 GB per device here, understates this total. Activation peaks, layer imbalance, and communication workspaces must fit in the remaining memory before this configuration is feasible.

Once the configuration fits in memory, its traffic still follows the same tiers, and each data-parallel group reduces only its tensor-and-pipeline shard of the 350 GB gradient buffer once per update. Gradient bucketing overlaps that reduction with backward computation, so its exposed cost depends on the computation remaining before the optimizer needs the reduced shard.

The last cost is scheduling rather than bandwidth, because a pipelined model is only efficient when enough microbatches are in flight to keep all stages busy. This remaining cost is the pipeline bubble developed in section 6.5.2. Applying its bubble fraction \(\frac{\PipelineParallelDegree-1}{\MicrobatchCount+ \PipelineParallelDegree - 1}\) to this hybrid layout, \(\PipelineParallelDegree=\) 16 stages with \(\MicrobatchCount=\) 32 microbatches waste \(\frac{15}{47} \approx 31.9\%\) of theoretical compute capacity, nearly one-third. Configuration C’s 4 stages at the same microbatch count waste roughly 9 percent. The deeper pipeline is chosen at this scale for two reasons. Its 16 stages spread the full training state to about 21.9 GB per device without optimizer sharding. Filling 16,384 GPUs with configuration C’s 32-GPU replicas would instead need 4× as many data-parallel replicas, which multiplies the global batch \(\DataParallelDegree \times \MicrobatchCount\times \MicrobatchSize\) by the same factor at the same per-replica schedule. The shallow pipeline spends global batch; the deep one spends bubble. The 1F1B scheduling that reclaims activation memory, and the larger microbatch counts it makes practical, drive the bubble fraction down asymptotically. Every added microbatch also enlarges the global batch, so the bubble target must be reconciled with the critical-batch constraint from section 6.4.6.

The Blackwell reference shows how a wider fast communication domain changes 3D parallelism choices. NVLink 5 provides 1.8 TB/s of bidirectional bandwidth per GPU, twice the Hopper reference’s capacity, which shortens the transfer term for a fixed payload but does not reduce message startup latency. Larger tensor-parallel (\(\TensorParallelDegree\)) groups, such as \(\TensorParallelDegree=\) 16 or \(\TensorParallelDegree=\) 32 across nodes, remain subject to topology and local-kernel efficiency. For a 1 trillion-parameter model, one candidate uses \(\TensorParallelDegree=\) 16 across two 8-GPU nodes joined by NVLink Switch and \(\PipelineParallelDegree=\) 8. Relative to the Hopper layout’s \(\TensorParallelDegree=\) 8 and \(\PipelineParallelDegree=\) 16, this preserves the model-partition product while halving stage count, and the bubble must be recomputed from the new stage and microbatch counts rather than assumed to halve. The 10 TB/s internal link joins Blackwell’s two dies behind one logical GPU, so those dies are not separate tensor-parallel ranks. Improving local bandwidth shifts pressure outward. Once intra-node exchanges bind less, the inter-node fabric that carries pipeline and data-parallel traffic, together with any expert AllToAll service, becomes the term to check against the next tier. The same placement constraints can be made operational through design-space search.

Example 6.1: Automated design space search
Scenario: An engineering team schedules a 175B-parameter model across 8,192 H100 GPUs (80 GB HBM each), eight times the 1,024-GPU reference cluster so that the pipeline and data-parallel degrees both have room to vary. The team navigates the multi-dimensional 3D-parallelism search space (TP \(\times\) PP \(\times\) DP).

Diagnosis: Candidate configurations must fit within 80 GB HBM and satisfy the modeled communication budget. The search evaluates TP \(\times\) PP \(\times\) DP factorizations and selects TP=8 GPUs, PP=8, and DP=128 as the best predicted MFU among configurations feasible under its assumptions. That result is a candidate for measurement, not proof of the best attainable runtime.

Systems lesson: This search favors TP=8 GPUs within the reference topology’s fast domain and PP=8, with 36.4 percent projected MFU. The result balances memory, local computation, transfers, and pipeline idle time within the model. A different topology, microbatch schedule, or kernel efficiency can change the preferred factorization.

Adjusting any one dimension shifts the pressure onto the other two, making the factorization TP \(\times\) PP \(\times\) DP a tightly coupled system rather than three independent knobs. A projected MFU such as this search’s still needs comparison with measured execution, and published runs report MFU well below peak that can fall as the same model moves to more GPUs (figure in Program goodput and its measure).

Checkpoint 6.4: Hybrid 3D parallelism

Verify your understanding of how parallelism strategies combine:

The three configurations and the worked layout tell one story. Pure data parallelism moves the full 350 GB gradient over the slowest tier and reaches only 15 percent scaling efficiency. Keeping tensor parallelism inside the NVLink domain cuts each rank’s share of that traffic by the tensor-parallel degree, and pipeline stages cut it again at the price of a bubble. Communication alone does not make a layout feasible, however. Each layout must also hold the full training state, which is why configurations B and C still need optimizer sharding and why the worked layout adds pipeline depth until the persistent state falls to about 21.9 GB per device. Hybrid parallelism is therefore the joint solution of a memory constraint, a bandwidth hierarchy, and a batch-size limit, and every axis added to satisfy one of them tightens another. Alignment training tests that joint solution hardest, because it asks one fleet to host several models with different memory and compute profiles at the same time.

Self-Check: Question

  1. In a 3D parallelism layout whose device count is the product of the data-, pipeline-, and tensor-parallel degrees, what hardware-matching principle dictates mapping tensor parallelism intra-node while pipeline parallelism spans inter-node links?

    1. Pipeline parallelism requires more memory bandwidth than NVLink can provide, forcing it onto InfiniBand
    2. Tensor parallelism requires only point-to-point transfers, making it insensitive to link latency
    3. Hierarchy-aware parallelism maps frequent TP exchanges to the fastest local fabric and PP boundary transfers to slower inter-node links.
    4. Data parallelism cannot execute across nodes that use tensor parallelism internally

  2. A 175B-parameter model uses mixed-precision Adam with 2-byte weights, 2-byte gradients, a 4-byte master copy, and two 4-byte moments per parameter. 6.7 Multi-Model Training for Alignment and RLHF

A cluster partitioned to train a single model cannot run post-training alignment in place, because reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) coordinates up to four distinct models inside a single training step. In proximal policy optimization (PPO), an active policy generates responses token by token, a preference-trained reward model scores them, a frozen reference copy constrains policy drift, and a learned value model estimates expected returns to guide the gradient update. Those roles carry sharply different memory footprints and alternate between memory-bandwidth-bound autoregressive generation and compute-bound backward passes. Direct preference optimization (DPO) sidesteps online generation and drops the separate reward and value models, trading live exploration for a simpler two-model loop. Designing an alignment cluster therefore starts by tallying which models, states, and execution phases must coexist on the fleet.

6.7.1 The multi-model coordination problem

Holding four models in accelerator memory at once multiplies the static state budget before a single prompt is even decoded, and only two of those models ever run a backward pass. In PPO (Schulman et al. 2017), as used in InstructGPT-style systems (Ouyang et al. 2022), the four roles differ sharply in execution mode and memory burden (table 7). In figure 21, the policy \(\Policy\) generates responses for scoring by the frozen reference and reward models and evaluation by the value model \(\CriticModel\). The penalized reward (\(\ResponseReward- \KlPenaltyCoefficient\,\text{KL}\)) subtracts a Kullback–Leibler divergence penalty, weighted by a coefficient \(\KlPenaltyCoefficient\) unrelated to link bandwidth \(\LinkBandwidth\), to discourage drift from the reference. Generalized advantage estimation (GAE) combines rewards and value estimates to judge how much better a response was than expected. Those estimates guide policy updates; the value model learns to predict returns, the cumulative discounted reward that follows each generated token. Reference and reward models remain forward-only in this loop, and shared-backbone implementations can combine roles without changing their distinct responsibilities.

Schulman, John, Filip Wolski, Prafulla Dhariwal, Alec Radford, and Oleg Klimov. 2017. “Proximal Policy Optimization Algorithms.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1707.06347, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.1707.06347.
Ouyang, L., J. Wu, X. Jiang, D. Almeida, C. L. Wainwright, P. Mishkin, C. Zhang, et al. 2022. “Training Language Models to Follow Instructions with Human Feedback.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 35: 27730–44. https://doi.org/10.52202/068431-2011.

The aggregate memory demand of the four-model PPO system dwarfs standard pretraining. A naive co-location of a 70B policy, 70B reference, 13B reward, and 13B value model requires approximately 1,494 GB of accelerator memory, before accounting for the KV caches and intermediate activations generated during sequence generation. A key-value (KV) cache stores attention keys and values for previously processed tokens so the policy can generate the next token without recomputing that state. It grows with the number and length of active sequences, creating a temporary memory budget distinct from weights and optimizer state. On H100 GPUs with 80 GB of HBM each, this system requires a minimum of 19 GPUs as an aggregate static-state capacity lower bound, including optimizer state and gradients. This division does not establish a feasible placement, because each phase must also fit its KV or activation peak, workspaces, and uneven shards. Replicated policy groups increase the total state further.

6.7.2 Infrastructure asymmetry between training and inference models

The defining infrastructure challenge of RLHF is not the total memory footprint but the asymmetry between the models’ compute profiles. The policy and value models require full backward passes with gradient computation, activation checkpointing, and optimizer updates, compute-intensive operations that benefit from tensor parallelism and high arithmetic intensity. The reference and reward models, by contrast, perform only forward passes over generated sequences. Their scoring passes do not require optimizer state, and they need not decode token by token. The policy’s rollout phase creates the autoregressive generation workload and its KV-cache budget. Separating scoring from generation matters because forward-only execution does not imply a single memory or compute profile.

The asymmetry creates a placement dilemma. Co-location permits devices to serve different phases, but inactive model state can occupy memory needed for the active phase. Dedicated pools allow each role to use a suitable execution configuration, but pools may idle while waiting for another role’s output. Separation also adds transfers of generated sequences, scores, and updated policy weights. The design must compare phase utilization and data movement rather than assume that either placement eliminates waste.

The PPO rollout phase introduces autoregressive dependencies absent from the teacher-forced pretraining baseline. The policy generates responses before sequence-level rewards guide its updates. At small active batches, decoding is often memory bandwidth bound because each new token requires another model pass. For a Llama-2-70B-style grouped-query attention policy generating 1,024 tokens for each of 256 active sequences, the per-token cache bytes of equation 10.5 put the generated-token portion at 85.9 GB. That portion alone already exceeds the 80 GB of HBM on one H100, and prompt tokens add KV state as well. This temporary cache must fit alongside the state live during rollout; its lifetime differs from training activations and optimizer state.

↪ The capacity wall this cache imposes on serving systems is developed in The KV cache wall and memory capacity.

RLHF systems address this asymmetry through temporal multiplexing. During the generation phase, the cluster behaves like an inference system: the policy model generates sequences while the reference model computes log-probabilities, and batching active sequences alongside their KV state determines how much useful work shares each weight read. During the training phase, the same fleet switches back to training mode and computes gradients through the policy and value models using the 3D parallelism configuration of section 6.6, where activation residency and gradient synchronization again govern the schedule. Because PPO alternates between inference-shaped rollout work across four model roles and training-shaped updates, teams often ask how much of that infrastructure overhead can be eliminated by reformulating the alignment objective itself.

6.7.3 Resource requirements for PPO versus DPO

Standard offline DPO (Rafailov et al. 2023) answers that question by removing the separate reward and value models, along with the online rollout phase, from the update loop, reformulating alignment as a supervised loss over offline preference pairs. The policy learns directly from preferred and dispreferred responses using their log-probabilities relative to a frozen reference; because those responses are already in the training data, the update requires neither autoregressive generation nor advantage estimation.

A DPO implementation that retains a resident reference has two model roles, the trainable policy and the frozen reference. For fixed preference pairs and a fixed reference, reference log-probabilities can instead be precomputed, removing that model from the repeated update loop. Offline DPO needs no autoregressive rollout during those updates. Variable lengths still require padding, packing, or length-aware batching, and policy training can use the same partitioning, accumulation, and overlap mechanisms as pretraining.

The infrastructure difference follows from how each method obtains feedback. Offline DPO learns from a fixed preference dataset, so the policy never receives feedback on new responses. PPO-style training scores responses from the evolving policy and can expose behavior the dataset lacks, provided the reward signal is reliable. The generation phase, the reward and value models, and the memory they occupy are the price of that online loop, so the choice is settled by achieved quality per unit of total resource cost.

To make the infrastructure trade-off concrete, consider aligning a 70B policy model using a policy/reference pool of 256 H100 GPUs (80 GB HBM each). PPO’s separate reward and value pools sit outside that allocation. The policy is a 70B-class model rather than the 175B reference, a size at which each role’s memory budget stays readable while the four PPO roles together still need at least 19 GPUs, more than one node.

Napkin Math 6.9: RLHF infrastructure budget: PPO vs. DPO
Problem: This alignment setup co-locates a 70B policy model with a 70B reference model and, for PPO, uses pools outside the 256-GPU policy/reference allocation for a 13B reward model and a 13B value model. Under 3D parallelism (TP=8 GPUs, PP=4, DP=8), how much memory resides on each policy GPU, and which separate model pools and generation-phase memory requirements does DPO remove?

PPO memory budget (per-GPU, with TP=8 GPUs, PP=4, DP=8 for policy)

The policy model under 3D parallelism with TP=8 GPUs and PP=4 distributes one replica’s 1,120 GB training state across 32 GPUs, yielding 35 GB per GPU. The reference model, requiring only 140 GB for inference, can instead be sharded across an additional 8 GPUs pool outside the policy allocation at 17.5 GB each. It can also be co-located with the policy GPUs at an additional 4.4 GB per GPU (140 GB / 32 GPUs). The 13B reward model adds 26 GB shared across its pool. The 13B value model in training mode adds approximately 208 GB (13B \(\times\) 16 bytes/param) across its pool.

Total static memory (one co-located policy + reference replica on 32 GPUs): 35 GB + 4.4 GB \(\approx\) 39.4 GB per GPU, leaving \(\sim\) 46.5 GB for activations and KV cache. With generation-phase KV caches for a rollout batch containing 256 sequences and 1,024 tokens inside one \(\Glyph{T} \Glyph{P}{\times}\Glyph{P} \Glyph{P}\) policy replica, each policy GPU must reserve an additional 85.9 GB/32 GPUs \(\approx\) 2.7 GB for its KV cache shard. The remaining \(\sim\) 43.8 GB is generation-phase headroom. Once rollout KV state is released, the update phase recovers the 46.5 GB headroom before allocating training activations and other runtime buffers. The phase peaks must be evaluated separately.

DPO memory budget (same parallelism configuration)

The policy model uses the same 35 GB per GPU. The reference model adds 4.4 GB per GPU (co-located). No reward model, no value model, no KV cache for generation.

On the policy GPUs, total static memory is 35 GB + 4.4 GB = 39.4 GB per GPU, identical to PPO’s co-located policy/reference footprint. Both have 46.5 GB available before update-phase activations and runtime buffers when PPO releases its rollout KV cache. DPO removes the separate generation peak and the reward/value model pools; the absence of rollout KV state alone does not establish larger update microbatches.

Throughput comparison

PPO alternates generation and parameter updates. If generation consumes 60 percent of an iteration in this illustrative schedule, updates occupy the remaining 40 percent; this time fraction is not MFU. Update-phase MFU must be multiplied by that fraction to report update computation over the whole iteration, using the same allocated hardware denominator. DPO eliminates online generation, but its measured throughput still depends on sequence lengths, reference scoring, parallelism, and memory use. The static-state comparison in table 8 is therefore kept separate from these schedule-dependent effects.

Systems insight: In this 13B reward/value-model scenario, DPO reduces the one-copy static-state budget by 15.7 percent by eliminating the reward and value models. It also removes online generation, but the throughput benefit requires a measured schedule. Infrastructure constraints therefore weigh on the choice between PPO and DPO as much as modeling preferences do. An organization with a limited GPU budget may prefer DPO because it restores the workload to a fixed-data training loop. If the PPO value model is policy-sized rather than 13B, the reduction is larger still, from 2,406 GB to 1,260 GB.

6.7.4 Sequence length variance and batching challenges

The throughput comparison in notebook 6.9 noted that even after DPO removes online generation, realized step time still depends on sequence-length variance. Pretraining often packs documents into fixed-length blocks, such as 2,048 or 4,096 tokens, whereas alignment batches combine prompts and responses whose lengths can span a 10–50\(\times\) range within a single minibatch. Those variable lengths directly govern activation residency, padding waste, and load balance across workers.

Length variance creates two interacting problems. Padding every sequence to the longest in a batch can leave most token positions unused. A batch of one 2,048-token sequence and fifteen 128-token sequences, padded to 2,048 tokens, fills only 3,968 of its 32,768 positions. Kernels that execute padded positions therefore spend about 88 percent of their work on padding, while kernels that skip them recover most of that waste. Dynamic batching groups similar lengths to reduce padding, but assignments across workers still need balancing. A worker processing longer sequences can have a larger activation peak and finish later, leaving other workers waiting for the required gradient contributions.

Sequence packing combines short sequences within a fixed-length input, with attention masking that keeps their contexts separate, and adaptive microbatching sizes each microbatch by token count or predicted memory. Alignment adds a constraint that pretraining lacks. The reduction must keep the objective’s own denominator, valid-token counts for a token-mean PPO loss and pair counts for standard DPO (Rafailov et al. 2023), so packing changes layout but not weighting. Balancing token work across workers then remains a performance goal rather than a correctness requirement.

Rafailov, Rafael, Archit Sharma, Eric Mitchell, Stefano Ermon, Christopher D. Manning, and Chelsea Finn. 2023. “Direct Preference Optimization: Your Language Model Is Secretly a Reward Model.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 36: 53728–41. https://doi.org/10.52202/075280-2338.

RLHF shows why no single parallelism strategy is sufficient by itself. The policy model may need tensor and pipeline parallelism, the reference and reward models behave like inference services, the value model may require training state, and the rollout data pipeline changes shape from step to step. The procedure that handles this case is the general one, which identifies the binding constraint first and then chooses the parallelism pattern that moves the least data while fitting the required state.

Self-Check: Question

    2. All four models in PPO must be trained with identical optimizer hyperparameters on the same GPU

    4. PPO eliminates backward passes entirely, turning distributed training into a stateless inference service

6.8 Parallelism Strategy Comparison

A parallelism strategy is useful only if it moves the binding constraint without creating a larger one. Assembling data, tensor, pipeline, and hybrid partitioning into a coherent decision procedure requires evaluating where each strategy relocates overhead across the cluster’s memory and interconnect hierarchy. For a new model, the decisive questions are which memory state fits, how often communication occurs, where the pipeline bubble appears, and which abstraction cost the engineering team can tolerate. Sequence, context, and expert parallelism sit beside the 3D core in table 9, and they remain subject to the same batch, memory, and topology constraints.

A decision path condenses the table, starting from memory fit and then asking which structure breaks the budget (figure 22). Before accepting any branch, check peak memory, global batch, communication service time, and load balance together; bandwidth is a feasibility constraint throughout the decision, not an afterthought.

Identifying the binding constraint comes before choosing an implementation stack, which is why both the strategy comparison and the decision path stay deliberately coarse. Dense large-model training and recommendation-style workloads motivated each of these partitioning strategies. The same constraint logic also reaches the federated edge case (table 10), where the bottleneck shifts from accelerator memory and fabric bandwidth to unreliable devices, privacy, and intermittent connectivity. A configuration chosen across these regimes still has to be expressed in software, and the way a framework expresses it decides which of the chapter’s budgets the engineer controls directly.

Lighthouse 6.3: Distributed archetype spectrum

Partitioning and coordination choices differ with the system’s primary bottleneck:

6.9 Frameworks and Runtime Contracts

A TP-8, PP-16, DP-128 layout that passes every feasibility check on paper can still hang at its first collective if two ranks disagree on group membership, or run slowly if gradient buckets become ready in an order the collective runtime cannot overlap. Choosing DDP, FSDP/ZeRO-style sharding, tensor parallelism, or pipeline parallelism is therefore also a decision about which constraints the runtime owns and which constraints the engineer must manage directly. Sparse workloads add a fourth contract, the parameter-server push/pull interface (Li et al. 2014) developed for Archetype B in section 6.5.6.

Li, M., D. G. Andersen, J. W. Park, A. J. Smola, A. Ahmed, V. Josifovski, J. Long, E. J. Shekita, and B.-Y. Su. 2014. “Scaling Distributed Machine Learning with the Parameter Server.” Proceedings of the 2014 International Conference on Big Data Science and Computing, 583–98. https://doi.org/10.1145/2640087.2644155.

The training framework defines process groups, gradient buckets, and the point in backpropagation when each reduction becomes ready; the collective runtime maps those reductions onto ring, tree, or hierarchical algorithms. The engineer must choose compatible group membership and bucket order and verify which communication remains exposed on the step’s critical path.

Production frameworks expose these same strategies under different names, which makes a translation aid useful when reading their documentation or configuration files. Four widely used stacks name the chapter’s strategies differently (table 11); the names change between releases, but the partition each one implements does not.

The training design therefore determines message size, readiness order, and frequency, while collective algorithms determine how those messages move. No framework abstraction changes those physical costs, so distributed-training efficiency ultimately depends on interconnect bandwidth, memory capacity, synchronization latency, and the useful overlap admitted by the computation graph. Confusing the simplicity of a framework API with exemption from those physical costs produces the recurring engineering mistakes below.

↩︎ Overlap of gradient exchange with backpropagation, the lever that takes communication off a step’s critical path, is established in Communication-Computation Overlap.

    1. Activation checkpointing
    2. ZeRO Stage 3 parameter gathering
    3. Temporal multiplexing

6.10 Fallacies and Pitfalls

Even when a framework makes switching from single-node DDP to multi-node execution a one-line configuration change, the underlying physics still governs the step time. In the GPT-2 scale-out of notebook 6.1 and notebook 6.2, moving the same model from one NVLink node to four Ethernet-connected nodes quadrupled the GPU count but cut parallel efficiency from 99.4 percent to 27.9 percent. The following misconceptions generalize that error, each treating one term of the step-time accounting as if it were the whole budget.

Fallacy: Linear speedup is achievable with sufficient engineering effort.

Amdahl’s Law limits speedup when a fixed serial component remains exposed. Synchronous gradient aggregation creates an update dependency, but some of its work can overlap backward computation. As section 6.4 shows, \(\ScalingEfficiency = 1/(1 + \DeviceCount(\CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount) + \SynchronizationTime(\DeviceCount) - \OverlappedTime)/\IronLawComputeTime)\) depends on the exposed overhead relative to useful compute as \(\DeviceCount\) changes. A 1,000-GPU allocation therefore does not by itself justify a 1,000\(\times\) speedup forecast. Local kernel efficiency, memory feasibility, communication, and convergence must be evaluated against the specified baseline. Linear scaling is a result to demonstrate for an operating range, not a return guaranteed by device count.

Pitfall: Optimizing MFU without considering scaling efficiency.

A single-node MFU measurement does not predict fleet throughput by itself. For a fixed workload and hardware peak convention, scaling efficiency compares measured distributed throughput with ideal replication of that single-node baseline. Multiplying the baseline MFU by this scaling efficiency gives distributed MFU; multiplying an already measured distributed MFU by the same scaling efficiency counts communication and synchronization losses twice. The chapter’s own numbers make the difference concrete. A single node running at 50 percent MFU, scaled with the 83 percent scaling efficiency of the overlapped TP-8 layout in section 6.6, delivers about 41 percent MFU across the fleet. A planner who measures that 41 percent on the full cluster and then applies the 83 percent again would budget for about 34 percent and buy capacity the job does not need. Capacity planning must state the device count and measurement window for each metric. Checkpointing, recovery, and replay require additional accounting only when those losses fall outside that window. Useful goodput must count retained model progress rather than repeated work, under the accounting boundaries of Accounting boundaries.

Fallacy: Hyperparameters tuned on small clusters transfer directly to large-scale training.

Engineers tune hyperparameters on 8-GPU workstations then deploy to 256-GPU clusters expecting identical behavior. At scale, convergence behavior changes. The most sensitive hyperparameter is learning rate. As section 6.3 explains, global batch increases with GPU count when local batch and accumulation depth remain fixed, so the learning-rate schedule must be revalidated. The linear scaling rule of section 6.4.5, which the one-hour ImageNet run established, suggests \(\ScaledLearningRate = \BaseLearningRate\times (\ScaledBatchSize/\BaseBatchSize)\), but this relationship holds only within bounds. As models scale, they eventually encounter the critical batch size of section 6.4.6, where adding more data per step yields diminishing returns in convergence.

Beyond the critical batch size, this relationship breaks down in a model-, data-, and optimizer-dependent way. A team that takes a small-cluster learning rate, multiplies it mechanically with GPU count, and jumps directly to a much larger global batch may see lower final quality or slower sample efficiency even though hardware throughput improved. Warmup schedules, weight decay adjustment, layer-wise optimizers such as LARS/LAMB, and careful momentum tuning can recover accuracy in some regimes, but require systematic experimentation at target scale. Organizations that skip these scaling studies waste thousands of GPU-hours on suboptimal runs.

Pitfall: Adding GPUs to data-parallel jobs without modeling communication.

More GPUs improve time to target only if the gain in processing rate outweighs communication overhead and any additional sample use. With fixed local batch size, adding data-parallel workers increases the global batch; fixing the global batch instead reduces local work and can expose more communication. The critical-batch analysis in section 6.4.6 supplies a workload-dependent statistical guide, not universal GPU-count or model-size thresholds. Compare complete learning curves and cost at the same target quality before committing to a larger allocation.

Fallacy: Memory capacity alone determines the parallelism strategy.

A model that exceeds device capacity needs a feasible partition, but memory fit does not select the fastest one. Tensor parallelism exchanges partial results within layers, while pipeline parallelism transfers stage-boundary activations and introduces scheduling bubbles. Their relative cost depends on layer shapes, microbatch count, placement, and overlap. Compare complete training-state and activation budgets together with measured time to target; an unsupported throughput percentage or a weights-only fit cannot settle the choice.

Pitfall: Applying FSDP or ZeRO without measuring efficiency trade-offs.

Engineers adopt FSDP universally after reading that it “reduces memory and enables larger models.” Sharding trades memory capacity for communication overhead: full-shard FSDP gathers parameters before layer execution and reduces gradient shards during backward, with another parameter gather when the forward pass resharded them. The relevant comparison begins with the complete training-state budget. Under the mixed-precision Adam convention established here, the 7B worked example exceeds single-device capacity before activations, so standard DDP cannot fit that state on an A100-80 GB. A weights-only fit does not establish a feasible training baseline. When the complete state, activations, and workspaces fit without sharding, DDP can avoid the repeated gathers; when they exceed the 80 GB capacity, the comparison must include a feasible alternative such as sharding or offload. FSDP provides value when it enables training at all or when the larger local batch it permits offsets its communication cost. ZeRO-Offload extends capacity through CPU memory but adds transfers of its own. The decision therefore requires measuring peak memory and exposed communication for the same workload and optimizer contract.

Fallacy: Parallelism overhead is roughly constant regardless of model size.

Engineers benchmark parallelism strategies on convenient small models then apply those conclusions to large-scale training. For dense transformers at fixed tokens per update, computation and gradient bytes both grow roughly proportionally with parameter count, as the earlier scaling analysis established. Larger parameter count alone therefore does not imply a better computation-to-communication ratio. Architecture, sequence length, local batch size, partition degree, and kernel efficiency determine how much useful work each rank performs; gradient representation and process-group membership determine how much it communicates. A larger model can also require a different partition that introduces new layer collectives or pipeline bubbles. Reliable strategy selection holds the workload and target quality explicit, then recomputes memory fit, local compute time, communication volume, and the available overlap window at the target scale. A small-model benchmark becomes transferable evidence only when those governing terms remain comparable.

Pitfall: Treating scaling efficiency as a property of the hardware alone.

A vendor benchmark might report 85 percent scaling efficiency at 1,024 GPUs, and procurement teams then plan capacity as if that number were a cluster constant. Scaling efficiency is a joint property of four things: the workload’s communication-to-computation ratio, the parallelism strategy (data, pipeline, tensor, hybrid), the network topology (fat-tree vs. torus vs. dragonfly), and the software stack (NCCL version, kernel fusion, overlap quality). Reference numbers from dense large-language-model benchmarks transfer poorly to other workloads and fabrics. They may overstate scaling for graph neural networks with irregular communication and for embedding-heavy recommendation models, whose AllToAll embedding exchanges and many small random lookups load the fabric differently from dense gradient AllReduce (section 6.5.6). They also say nothing about a commodity-Ethernet placement like the GPT-2 scale-out, which lost most of its parallel efficiency on leaving the node. Capacity planning should use measured scaling on the target workload, not a vendor hero benchmark.

Fallacy: Gradient accumulation is free.

Gradient accumulation can reduce synchronization from every microstep to once per window of \(\AccumulationStepCount\) microsteps, eliminating \((\AccumulationStepCount-1)/\AccumulationStepCount\) of those synchronization events when the implementation defers reduction. The saving has costs. A resident gradient buffer remains live throughout the accumulation window; for the 7B model, the FP16 buffer occupies 14 GB. Retaining graphs across microsteps or increasing the local batch size can add activation pressure. Accumulating eight microsteps makes updates 8\(\times\) less frequent per processed microstep, but wall-clock spacing also depends on the communication saved. Larger effective batches can change convergence, and narrow accumulation formats can lose small contributions or overflow. The dtype, loss normalization, and reduction schedule must therefore be validated at the intended batch size. Accumulation trades communication frequency against update spacing, memory residency, and numerical stability.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A team achieves 50 percent Model Flops Utilization (MFU) on a single node, scales to 1,024 GPUs, and measures a scaling efficiency \((\eta_{\text{scaling}})\) of 50 percent on their communication-heavy workload. What is the cluster’s realized model-FLOP rate during distributed execution as a fraction of peak hardware compute?

    1. 25 percent of peak compute (delivering 256 GPU-equivalents of model computation)
    2. 50 percent of peak compute (delivering 512 GPU-equivalents of useful work)
    3. 100 percent of peak compute (delivering 1,024 GPU-equivalents of useful work)
    4. 12.5 percent of peak compute (delivering 128 GPU-equivalents of useful work)

  2. A procurement team plans capacity for a graph neural network workload using a vendor benchmark that reports 85 percent scaling efficiency at 1,024 GPUs for large-language-model training. Explain why this figure cannot be treated as a property of the cluster and what the team should measure instead.

  3. Why is gradient accumulation NOT a cost-free substitute for true physical scale-out even though it reduces gradient AllReduce communication frequency by a factor of \(k\)?

    1. It is mathematically incompatible with the Adam optimizer
    2. It keeps a live gradient buffer resident, spaces optimizer updates by \(k\) microsteps, and requires appropriate accumulation precision and normalization
    3. It requires dedicated InfiniBand links between CPU and GPU for each microstep
    4. It forces batch normalization layers to synchronize across all nodes on every forward pass

  4. The largest global batch size beyond which adding more samples per step yields diminishing reductions in the number of steps needed to reach target quality is called the ____.

  5. True or False: If a 7B-parameter model fits comfortably in single-GPU memory with high compute utilization under standard DDP, universally enabling ZeRO-3 / FSDP full sharding will always increase training throughput by reducing per-GPU memory consumption.
